DEDICATED TO THE TEXAS LAW ENFORCEMENT FOUNDATION 

The Texas Law Enforcement Foundation is "dedicated to enlisting the aid of good citizens in support of good law enforcement." The Texas Law Enforcement Officer has been written for the peace officer as a contribution toward better law enforcement in Texas. To the Foundation, its ideals and objectives, this book is dedicated.

Imp. 875

Program of the Texas Law 

Enforcement Foundation I. To call public attention to the great cost of crime in terms of wasted lives, violent deaths, human indignities and property loss and the great saving that could be effected through crime prevention and better enforcement. (Crime in Texas cost $989 million in 1955.)

II. To operate and maintain a CRIMEMOBILE-LAB fitted out with the latest criminal detection devices including lie detector, fingerprinting, police records and other exhibits counteracting popular appeal of desperadoes. Itinerary of this vehicle would be by invitation of school, scouting or other officials including local police. (This 55-foot tractor-trailer combination is a rolling classroom designed to impress our young people with the ingenuity and efficiency of modern police techniques.)

III. To publish information bulletins for all law enforcement officials and interested citizens, this bulletin to contain latest information on crime prevention, control, penology, court decisions, and to recognize outstanding work on the part of law enforcement officials.

IV. To implement, assist and encourage existing training schools conducted by all law enforcement agencies, to work with these established agencies to broaden these programs to reach more officers, to sponsor training courses on a local and county level in cooperation with county and district attorneys for those officers who are unable to attend district or statewide training courses.

V. To study salary and personnel problems of Texas law enforcement officials with the view of assisting in the correction of inequities and the payment of compensation in line with the constant personal risks involved.

VI. To encourage the establishment of a full four-year college course in criminology in some Texas university and to provide scholarships and other assistance to selected students. 

VII. To establish a working library on criminology to be available to every police department, sheriff's office and prosecu-

PROGRAM OF THE TEXAS LAW ENFORCEMENT FOUNDATION V 

tor, a historical library on criminology, and the wider use of criminology materials in high school libraries to encourage high school students to enter the law enforcement profession. 

VIII. To sponsor annual awards to outstanding law enforcement officials and lay citizens on a statewide and local basis.

INTRODUCTION 

The law applies in a special way to the law enforcement officer. It grants the officer his authority, and at the same time, limits the methods by which he may enforce the law. This is considered a necessary safeguard for the rights of the lawabiding citizen; but it is often a loophole for the criminal, and a headache for the peace officer.

The law imposes many requirements on the peace officer which appear difficult to meet. Yet he still must do his job. When may an officer act as he sees fit? And after the criminal is behind bars, what will keep him there? A slight mistake on the part of an officer-a defective writ, an overzealous search-will cause the learned judge to shuffle through a batch of appellate briefs, dust off an aged law book, and write a lofty court opinion on constitutional rights against unreasonable searches, and due process of law. Then the defendant will be freed after the officer has risked his life to put him in jail. It is not the fault of the judge; the law applies to him as well as to anyone else, including the most incorrigible, social misfit we call a criminal. 

Regardless of the loopholes open to the law-evaders, justice can still be done. The peace officer must know how to use the tools of his trade, not merely his alertness and his weapon, but the law itself. When the officer knows how to enforce the law as well as the violator knows how to enforce his legal rights, the scales of justice are once more balanced in favor of the officer. 

In a sense the peace officer must be enforcer, prosecutor, and judge all at the same time. He must make the right decision at the right moment in order to bring himself within the law on the one hand, and to determine whether or not his suspect has placed himself outside the law on the other. In short, the officer must know his law. He must know it like a surgeon knows his anatomy, like a navigator knows his maps-like any expert professional knows his business.

The Texas Law Enforcement Officer has been written to provide the officer with the fundamentals of Texas law and procedure. The need for more adequate legal training has long been recognized; but, information on the law has always been buried in law libraries of numberless volumes, inaccessible to the peace officer. Now, The Texas Law Enforcement Officer has brought the essentials of many volumes into one compact hand-

viii 

INTRODUCTION 

book written especially for the peace officer. It treats the most common legal problems which daily confront the officer. 

What does the law say? The chapter on The Texas Penal 

Code gives the text of the most important criminal laws which comprise more than seventeen hundred statutes enacted and amended by the legislature over the past thirty years. In addition, the interpretation of these laws from actual case decisions has been given in brief, pointed excerpts. The chapters on 

Arrest, and Criminal Writs and Process summarize the complicated but commonly met portions of the Code of Criminal Procedure. The section on Search and Seizure gives detailed studies of actual cases involving searches and seizures, which, it is hoped, will aid the officer in determining when he is permitted to make an arrest and search that will stand up in court. 

The chapter on Drug Addiction and Narcotics Laws should be of particular interest to Texas peace officers in view of the recent congressional investigations of the nationwide narcotics problems. Today the officer must understand the addict, his problem and his threat to the state, and know how to deal with him legally.

Delinquent juveniles have produced a major crime problem in the United States. These youngsters, for the most part, have no propensity for crime, and the law does not consider their conduct criminal in the legal sense of the term. What is the legal status of juveniles in Texas under the Juvenile Delinquency Act?

How does the law deal with youthful offenders? These questions are answered in the chapter entitled Juvenile Delinquency.

Rules of evidence in the courtroom are not the direct concern of the peace officer, but unless the officer has a view toward complying with these rules when he is at the scene of a crime, the prosecuting attorney cannot successfully try his case. A better knowledge of the rules and of the different kinds of evidence, will better equip the officer to gather and to sort the evidence which he meets firsthand. A chapter on Rules of Evidence in Texas Courts is provided to give the peace officer a working knowledge of this branch of law enforcement. 

Part III of the handbook sets out the latest Rules of Civil Procedure and other related statutes which directly concern officers who spend a considerable amount of their time with the task of process-serving connected with civil litigation.

INTRODUCTION 

ix 

A special feature of The Texas Law Enforcement Officer is the appendix which contains sample examinations for peace. officers. These questions are similar to actual examinations used. in police academies in the State of Texas and civil service tests. 

For the young man interested in law enforcement as a career, the questions (with answers and page references to the text) should provide him with an introduction to his course of study. 

For the practicing peace officer it is unnecessary to impress upon him the importance of the continued study of the law he enforces. Promotions in the law enforcement field are no longer based on political influence but solely upon merit. The man who studies and knows his job better, gets faster promotions. 

It should be remembered that this text cannot answer every legal question which may confront the officer. Moreover, the law is apt to change, and interpretations of the law are sometimes vague and contradictory. All legal questions in this book have come from the statutes and the case decisions which have construed these statutes. The exact meaning of any given decision or statute is often a matter of opinion. The Texas Law Enforcement Officer presents the fundamentals and elaborates only where a set of circumstances has been passed upon by the courts. 

When in doubt, the peace officer should not fail to consult the prosecuting attorney.

PART I  CHAPTER 1 Peace Officers GENERAL DESIGNATION AND DUTIES Art. 36 C.C.P. Who Are Peace Officers. 

The following are "peace officers": the sheriff and his deputies, constable, the marshal or policemen of an incorporated town or city, the officers, noncommissioned officers and privates of the state ranger force, and any private person specially appointed to execute criminal process.

Art. 37 C.C.P. Duties and Powers. 

It is the duty of every peace officer to preserve the peace within his jurisdiction. To effect this purpose, he shall use all lawful means. He shall, in every case where he is authorized by the provisions of this Code, interfere without warrant to prevent or to suppress crime. He shall execute all lawful process issued to him by any magistrate or court. He shall give notice to some magistrate of all offenses committed within his jurisdiction, where he has good reason to believe there has been a violation of the penal law. He shall arrest offenders without warrant in any case where he is authorized by law, in order that they may be taken before the proper magistrate or court and be tried.

Art. 38 C.C.P. May Summon Aid. 

Whenever a peace officer meets with resistance in discharging any duty imposed upon him by law, he shall summon a sufficient number of citizens of his county to overcome the resistance; and all persons summoned are bound to obey.

SHERIFF Art. 41 C.C.P. Conservator of the Peace. 

Each sheriff shall be a conservator of the peace in his county, and shall arrest all offenders against the laws of the state, in his view or hearing, and take them before the proper court for examination or trial. He shall quell and suppress all assaults and batteries, affrays, insurrections and unlawful assemblies.

He shall apprehend and commit to jail all offenders, until an examination or trial can be had.

Art. 6877 R.C.S. Unfinished Business. 

When any sheriff or any constable shall from any cause vacate his office, all unfinished business whatsoever in his hands shall be transferred to his successor, and be completed by him in the same manner as if commenced by himself. 

The sheriff and deputies may summon the aid of the county when they have met with resistance in the execution of any legal process. (Art. 6876 R.C.S.) The sheriff or constable has the duty to seize and impound stock running at large on any outside premises. (Art. 6965 R.C.S.) Art. 4.03 (Election Code) Writs of Election. 

The writs of election and copies of the form of returns shall be delivered to the sheriff of the county, who shall, previous to the day of election, deliver the same to the presiding officer of each election precinct in which any general or special election is ordered to be held, and in case there is no presiding officer in any such election precinct, the same shall be delivered to the qualified voter of such election precinct who resides at or nearest to the voting place in such precinct. The county judge shall, fifteen days before any general or special election, notify each presiding judge in writing of his duty to hold said election in that precinct.

The sheriff has power to appoint deputies in accordance with 

Art. 6869 R.C.S. et seq. 

Art. 42 C.C.P. Custody of Prisoners. 

When a prisoner is committed to jail by a warrant from a magistrate or court, he shall be placed in jail by the sheriff. It is a violation of duty on the part of any sheriff to permit a defendant so committed to remain out of jail, except that he may, when a defendant is committed for want of bail, or when he arrests in a bailable case, give the person arrested a reasonable time to procure bail; but, he shall so guard the accused as to prevent escape.

Art. 43 C.C.P. Report as to Prisoners. 

At each term of any court having criminal jurisdiction, the sheriff shall give notice to the district or county attorney as to all prisoners in his custody and of the authority under which he detains them.

Art. 44 C.C.P. Deputy. 

Wherever a duty is imposed by this Code upon the sheriff, the same duty may lawfully be performed by his deputy. When

PEACE OFFICERS 

3 there is no sheriff in a county, the duties of that office, as to all proceedings under the criminal law, devolve upon the officer who, under the law, is empowered to discharge the duties of sheriff, in case of vacancy in the office. 

Art. 5115 R.C.S. Jails Provided. 

The commissioners court shall provide safe and suitable jails for their respective counties, and shall cause the same to be kept in good repair. They shall see that the jails of their respective counties are kept in a clean and healthy condition, properly ventilated, and not overcrowded with prisoners, and that they are furnished with clean and comfortable mattresses and blankets sufficient for the comfort of the prisoners. When there is no jail in a county, the sheriff may rent a suitable house and employ guards, the expense to be paid by the proper county. 

Art. 5116 R.C.S. Sheriff and Jailer. 

Each sheriff is the keeper of the jail of his county. He shall safely keep therein all prisoners committed thereto by lawful authority, subject to the order of the proper court, and shall be responsible for the safekeeping of such prisoners. The sheriff may appoint a jailer to take charge of the jail, and supply the wants of those therein confined; but in all cases the sheriff shall exercise a supervision and control over the jail.

Art. 5117 R.C.S. Marshal May Use Jail. 

Sheriffs and jailers shall receive into their jails such prisoners as may be delivered or tendered to them by any United

States marshal or his deputy for any district of Texas, and shall safely keep such prisoners until they are demanded by such marshal or his deputy, or are discharged by due course of law. 

The marshal by whose authority such prisoners are received and kept shall be directly and personally liable to the sheriff or jailer for the jail fees and all other expenses of the keeping of such prisoners, such fees and expenses to be estimated according to the laws regulating the same in other cases. 

Art. 5118 R.C.S. Prisoner Sent to Another Jail. 

A county to which a prisoner is sent, for want of a safe jail in the proper county, may by suit recover from the county from which such prisoner was sent a sum not exceeding 75 cents per day on account of the expense attending the safekeeping of such prisoner. 

Art. 5118a R.C.S. Commutation for Good Conduct; Forfeiture of 

Commutation; Record.

In order to encourage county jail discipline, a distinction may be made in the term of prisoners so as to extend to all such as are orderly, industrious, and obedient, comforts and privileges

according to their deserts; the reward to be bestowed on prisoners for good conduct shall consist of such relaxation of strict county jail rules, and extension of social privileges as may be consistent with proper discipline. Commutation of time for good conduct, industry, and obedience may be granted the inmates of each county jail by the sheriff in charge. A deduction in time not to exceed one-third of the original sentence may be made from the term or terms of sentences when no charge of misconduct has been sustained against the prisoner. A prisoner under two or more cumulative sentences shall be allowed commutation as if they were all one sentence. For such sustained charge of misconduct in violation of any rule known to the prisoner (including escape or attempt to escape) any part or all of the commutation which shall have accrued in favor of the prisoner to the date of said misconduct may be forfeited and taken away by the sheriff. No other time allowance or credits in addition to the commutation of time for good conduct herein provided for may be deducted from the term or terms of sentences. The sheriff shall keep or cause to be kept, a conduct record in card or ledger form and a calendar card on each inmate showing all forfeitures of commutation time and the reasons therefor.

THE TEXAS RANGERS Art. 4413 (11) R.C.S. The Texas Rangers. 

1. The Texas Ranger Force and its personnel, property, equipment, and records, are under the jurisdiction of the Department of Public Safety, and are designated as the Texas

Rangers, and as such, constitute the above-mentioned division of the department. 

2. The Texas Rangers shall consist of six captains, one headquarters sergeant, and such number of privates as may be authorized by the legislature, except in cases of emergency when the commission, with the consent of the governor, shall have authority to increase the force to meet extraordinary conditions. 

3. The compensation of the officers shall be such as allowed by the legislature. 

4. The officers shall be clothed with all the powers of peace officers, and shall aid in the execution of the laws. They shall have authority to make arrests, and to execute process in criminal cases; and in civil cases when specially directed by the judge of a court of record; and in all cases shall be governed by the laws regulating and defining the powers and duties of sheriffs when in the discharge of similar duties; except that they shall have the power and shall be authorized to make arrests and to execute all process in criminal cases in any county in

PEACE OFFICERS 

5 the state. All officers operating by virtue of this Act shall have the authority to make arrests, as directed by warrants, and without a warrant under the conditions now authorized by law, and also in all cases when the alleged offender is traveling on a railroad, in a motor vehicle, airplane or boat. When any of said force shall arrest any person charged with a criminal offense, they shall forthwith convey said person to the county where he so stands charged, and shall deliver him to the proper officer, taking his receipt therefor. All necessary expenses thus incurred shall be paid by the state. 

5. Special Rangers: The commission shall have authority to appoint such number of special rangers as may be deemed advisable, not to exceed three hundred in number; such rangers shall not have any connection with any ranger company or highway motor patrol, but they shall at all times be subject to the orders of the commission and the governor for special duty to the same extent as the other law enforcing officers provided for in this Act; such special rangers, however, shall not have the authority to enforce any laws except those designed to protect life and property, and such rangers are specially denied the authority to enforce any laws regulating the use of the state highways by motor truck and motor buses and other motor vehicles. 

Such rangers shall not receive any compensation from the state for their services, and before the issuance of the commission each such ranger shall enter into a good and sufficient bond executed by a surety company authorized to do business in Texas in the sum of $2,500, approved by the director, indemnifying all persons against damages accruing as the result of any illegal or unlawful acts on the part of such special ranger. All special ranger commissions shall expire on January 1 of the odd year after appointment, and the director can revoke any special ranger commission at any time for cause, and such officer shall be designated in the commission as special ranger. 

Provided further that the commission shall not issue more than ten commissions to special rangers for employment by any one person, firm or corporation at any one time, except during an emergency, when in the opinion of the commission it is necessary in the interest of the public justice to permit the employment of more than ten. 

6. In the execution of the laws of the state under the Department of Public Safety, the officials shall in all cases where it becomes necessary to seize property and destroy the same, to proceed as now provided by law; and all property so seized shall be stored and a list thereof presented to a district judge in the district where such property is seized, who shall dispose of same

in the mode and manner now provided by Arts. 5112, 5113, and 

5114 R.C.S.

Any official disregarding these provisions shall by virtue thereof be subject to removal from office. 

THE TEXAS HIGHWAY PATROL Art. 4413 (12) R.C.S. The Texas Highway Patrol. 

The Texas Highway Patrol Division of the Department of 

Public Safety consists of the chief patrol officer who is the executive officer of the patrol and such number of captains, sergeants, and privates as may be authorized by the legislature, and such administrative and clerical help as may be determined by the commission. (Sec. 2.) Officers of the Texas Highway 

Patrol are clothed with all the powers and authority which is in this Act or otherwise by law given to members of the Texas 

Ranger Force. (Sec. 4.)

SPECIAL POLICE AND CITY MARSHAL Art. 995 R.C.S. Special Police Force in Cities. 

Whenever the mayor deems it necessary, in order to enforce the laws of the city, or to avert danger or to protect life or property, in case of riot or any outbreak or calamity or public disturbance, or when he has reason to fear any serious violation of law or order, or any outbreak or any other danger to said city, or the inhabitants thereof, he shall summon into service as a special police force, all or as many of the citizens as in his judgment may be necessary. Such summons may be by proclamation or other order addressed to the citizens generally, or those of any ward of the city, or subdivision thereof, or may be by personal notification. Such special police force while in service, shall be subject to the orders of the mayor, shall perform such duties as he may require, and shall have the same power while on duty as the regular police force of said city. 

Art. 999 R.C.S. City Marshal. 

The marshal of the city shall be ex-officio chief of police, and may appoint one or more deputies which appointment shall only be valid upon the approval of the city council. Said marshal shall, in person or by deputy, attend upon the corporation court while in session, and shall promptly and faithfully execute all writs and process issued from said court.

He shall have like power, with the sheriff of the county, to execute warrants; he shall be active in quellng riots, disorder and disturbance of the peace within the city limits and shall take into custody all persons so offending against the peace of the city and shall have authority to take suitable and sufficient

PEACE OFFICERS 

7 bail for the appearance before the corporation court of any person charged with an offense against the ordinance or laws of the city. It shall be his duty to arrest, without warrant, all violators of the public peace, and all who obstruct or interfere with him in the execution of the duties of his office or who shall be guilty of any disorderly conduct or disturbance whatever; to prevent a breach of the peace or preserve quiet and good order, he shall have authority to close any theater, ballroom or other place or building of public resort. In the prevention and suppression of crime and arrest of offenders, he shall have, possess and execute like power, authority, and jurisdiction as the sheriff. 

He shall perform such other duties and possess such other powers and authority as the city council may by ordinance require and confer, not inconsistent with the constitution and laws of this state. The marshal shall give such bond for the faithful performance of his duties as the city council may require. He shall receive a salary or fees of office, or both, to be fixed by the city council. The governing body of any city or town having less than three thousand inhabitants according to the preceding federal census, may by an ordinance, dispense with the office of marshal, and at the same time by such ordinance confer the duties of said office upon any peace officer of the county, but no marshal elected by the people shall be removed from his office under the provisions of this article. 

Art. 999a R.C.S. Marshal May Be Dispensed With. 

The governing body of any city or town operating under the general laws having less than five thousand inhabitants according to the last preceding federal census, may by ordinance, dispense with the office of marshal, and at the same time by such ordinance confer the duties of said office upon a city police officer to be appointed as the city council shall direct. 

JUVENILE OFFICER Art. 5142 R.C.S. Qualifications, Duties, Appointment and Removal of Juvenile Officers. 

There may be appointed, in the manner hereinafter provided, discreet persons of good moral character to serve as juvenile officers, for periods not to exceed two years from date of appointment.

Such officers shall have authority and it shall be their duty to make investigations of all cases referred to them as such by such board; to be present in court and to represent the interest of the juvenile when the case is heard, and to furnish to the court and such board any information and assistance as such board may require, and to take charge of any child before and

after the trial and to perform such other services for the child as may be required by the court or said board, and such juvenile officers shall be vested with all the power and authority of police officers or sheriffs incident to their offices. 

The clerk of the court shall, when practicable, notify such juvenile officer when any juvenile is to be brought before the court. It shall be the duty of such juvenile officer to make investigation of any such case, to be present in court to represent the interest of the juvenile when the case is tried, to furnish to such court such information and assistance as the court may require and to take charge of any juvenile before and after the trial as the court may direct.

GAME, FISH AND OYSTER COMMISSION Art. 905 P.C. Commissioner to Enforce Game Law. 

The Game, Fish and Oyster Commissioner and his deputies shall have the same power and authority as sheriffs to serve criminal processes in connection with cases growing out of the violations of this chapter, shall have the same power as sheriffs to require aid in executing such process, and shall be entitled to receive the same fees as are provided by law for sheriffs in misdemeanor cases.

Said commissioner or any of his deputies may arrest without a warrant any person found by them in the act of violating any of the laws for the protection and propagation of game, wild birds or fish, and take such person forthwith before a magistrate having jurisdiction. Such arrests may be made on Sunday, and in which case the person arrested shall be taken before a magistrate having jurisdiction, and proceeded against as soon as may be, on a week day following the arrest.

Art. 906 P.C. Deputy Commissioners to Enforce Law. 

It is hereby made a special duty of the Game, Fish and Oyster Commissioner to enforce the statutes of this state for the protection and preservation of wild game and wild birds; and to bring, or cause to be brought, actions and proceedings in the name of the State of Texas, to recover any and all fines and penalties provided for in the laws now in force, or which may hereafter be enacted, relating to wild game and wild birds. Said 

Game, Fish and Oyster Commissioner may make complaint and cause proceedings to be commenced against any person for violating any of the laws for the protection and propagation of game or birds without the sanction of the county attorney of the county in which such proceedings are commenced; and in such cases he shall not be required to furnish security for costs.

PEACE OFFICERS

INVESTIGATORS OF DISTRICT OR COUNTY ATTORNEYS Art. 326k-6 R.C.S. Arrests and Service of Process in Criminal Cases. 

Any and all investigators appointed by a district attorney or county attorney where said county attorney performs the duties of a district attorney or criminal district attorney, as provided by law, shall have the same authority as the sheriff of the county to make arrests anywhere in the county, and to serve anywhere in the county, warrants, capiases, subpoenas in criminal cases, and all other processes in criminal cases issued by any district court, county court, or justice court in the state, but such investigators shall not be under the authority and direction of the sheriff, and shall only be under the authority and direction of the said district attorney or county attorney where said county attorney performs the duties of a district attorney or criminal district attorney; and such investigators shall not be allowed to draw any fees of any character for performing such duties. (Sec. 1.)

TEXAS LIQUOR CONTROL BOARD Art. 666-7b P.C. Inspectors and Representatives. 

All inspectors and representatives of the Texas Liquor Con trol Board shall subscribe to the constitutional oath of office which shall be filed in the office of the board. The board or administrator is empowered to commission such number of its inspectors and representatives which it deems necessary to en force the provisions of this Act. (Texas Liquor Control Act.) 

Such commissioned inspectors and representatives shall have all the powers of a peace officer coextensive with the boundaries of this state. Such commissioned inspectors and representatives shall make and execute such bond as may be required by the board.

WEIGHTS AND MEASURES INSPECTORS Art. 5709 R.C.S. Jurisdiction. 

The jurisdiction of all state sealers, deputy sealers and inspectors appointed by the commissioner shall be coextensive with the limits of the state and they shall have a right to inspect weights and measures in any and all districts or localities designated by the commissioner. The jurisdiction of all local sealers of weights and measures appointed by the governing body of any city in this state shall be coextensive with the limits of said city.

Art. 5710 R.C.S. Same Power as Peace Officer. 

The commissioner, his deputy, sealers or inspectors and all local sealers and their deputies in the performance of their official duties, shall have the same power as peace officers in this state.

AIRPORT POLICE Art. 46d-7 R.C.S. Airport Police. 

A municipality, which has established or acquired or which may hereafter establish or acquire an airport or air navigation facility, is authorized to adopt, amend and repeal such reasonable ordinances, resolutions, rules, regulations and orders as it shall deem necessary for the management, government and use of such airport or air navigation facility under its control, whether situated within or without the territorial limits of the municipality. For the enforcement thereof, the municipality may, by ordinance or resolution, as may by law be appropriate, appoint airport guards or police, with full police powers, and fix penalties, within the limits prescribed by law, for the violation of the aforesaid ordinances, resolutions, rules, regulations and orders.

CHAPTER Drug Addiction and Narcotics Laws INTRODUCTION 

The illegal use of narcotics, which has risen sharply since World War I, is a prime factor in the development of crime. 

Because of the severity of the problem in the United States at the present time, it is important that all law enforcement officers have a thorough knowledge of the narcotics situation. 

This chapter contains a general survey of drug addiction and its relationship to crime. Some significant findings of the 1955 congressional investigative hearings on narcotics are included together with an explanation of the present federal and state narcotics laws.

STATISTICS 

Man has always sought means of escape from physical pain and mental suffering. Alcohol is the substance most commonly used for this purpose because it is easily obtainable and not prohibited by law. Narcotics have been less commonly used because of their unavailability and high cost, but are now relatively easy to obtain on the illicit market. Opium was known and used by the ancients. In the last few centuries, the alkiloids of opium -morphine and heroin-have come into common use. Mexico and a number of Asiatic countries are now the principal sources of opium and heroin drugs sold on the illicit market in the United States. Today, drug addicts have begun to use nearly all forms of drugs including synthetic products.

The total number of narcotics users in the United States is estimated at between 50,000 and 60,000 or an incidence of about one in 3,000 of the population. Of this number, approximately 

10,000 are teen-agers. Approximately fifty per cent of the addicts are between the ages of twenty-one and thirty. Texas rates seventh in the United States in the number of drug addicts, sixth in the number of arrests, and fourth in the number of convictions for illegal possession and use of narcotics. Texas has the greatest number of violations of the marihuana drug laws. 

CONGRESSIONAL INVESTIGATION OF ILLICIT NARCOTICS TRAFFIC 

During the latter part of 1955, the United States Senate authorized the first nationwide investigation of the illicit narcotics traffic, including foreign sources, narcotic smuggling operations, drug addiction, treatment of drug addicts, and related

matters. Some of the significant findings of this investigation point out the severity of the narcotics problem in this country. 

The investigators found that the United States has more narcotics addicts than any other country in the Western World. 

Despite the efforts made to curb the drug problem, illicit narcotics traffic has trebled since World War II. An average of 

2,000 persons are arrested in the United States each month on narcotics charges. The traffic now costs over $500,000,000 per year. Drug addiction is responsible for approximately fifty per cent of all crimes committed in the larger metropolitan areas and twenty-five per cent of all reported crimes in the nation. 

The committee on narcotics found that addicts spread the habit with cancerous rapidity to their families and associates. 

Over ninety per cent of the addicts acquire the habit through friends and associates.

It was also determined that Mexico and Red China are primary sources of opiate drugs, particularly heroin, the most frequently used drug among American addicts. Subversion through drug addiction is an established aim of Communist China. Ninety per cent of the overland smuggling of heroin and marihuana is across the Mexican border into Texas, California, and Arizona.

Heroin purchased abroad today for $3,000 will bring $300,000 when finally cut, packaged, and sold in the United States. 

TYPES OF NARCOTICS AND HOW THEY ARE USED BY ADDICTS 

The term "narcotics" refers generally to the following drugs: 

1. Opium and all its derivatives including morphine, apomorphine, heroin, dilaudid, paregoric, laudanum, codeine, pantopon, and metopon. 

2. The synthetic equivalents of opiate drugs including demerol and methadone. 

3. Cocaine, a derivative of coca leaves, and hashish which is made from Indian hemp and called marihuana. 

4. Mescaline or peyote cactus which comes from the buttons of the peyote cactus which grows in the southwestern 

United States and Mexico. 

5. Isonipecaine. 

6. Papaverine.

Narcotic drugs commonly used in the United States can be divided into two main groups: "stimulants" and "depressants."

The stimulants tend to excite the nervous system and to keep the user awake. The depressants produce drowsiness and a sense of ease and contentment in the individual. 

The most common stimulants are cocaine, benzedrine, and mescaline. Cocaine is taken as a white powder and is known as

"snow" by addicts. They sniff it through the nose or inject it in the veins with a hypodermic needle. It produces an almost immediate feeling of intense pleasure. Fatigue disappears and the user has a feeling of superiority and increased ability. This ecstasy lasts only a short time, and is followed by a period of depression and nervous apprehension which can be relieved only by taking another injection or "shot." Hallucinations and paranoiac delusions also appear during cocaine intoxication. In this state, addicts may become dangerous.

Benzedrine and barbiturates are usually taken orally to gether to derive the desired effect. Hallucinations during benzedrine intoxication are similar to those of cocaine intoxication. 

Mescaline is used mostly by the Indians in the southwestern United States and in Mexico. It is taken orally in the form of the buttons of the peyote cactus. Hallucinations following mescaline ingestion are in the form of vivid colors and patterns and differ only slightly from those resulting from cocaine or benzedrine intoxication.

The depressants include opium and all its derivatives (morphine, heroin, dilaudid, codeine, dihydrocodeinone, and metopon), the synthetic analgesics (methadone and demerol), and all the hypnotics and sedatives (chloral, paraldehyde, barbiturates, and marihuana.)

American addicts seldom use opium, but there is a great deal of illicit use of its main derivatives, morphine and heroin. 

Both drugs are powerfully addicting powders and are sold illicitly by peddlers mainly in capsules or in flat packages. Heroin is the most frequently used opiate among American addicts, and it is considered more dangerous than morphine. Its manufacture and sale were prohibited by federal law more than twenty-five years ago, and it is now available only on the illegal market. 

Heroin is taken either by sniffing through the nose or by injection with a hypodermic needle. Heroin is popular with drug peddlers because it can be easily diluted, which is the way by which peddlers make money from the sale of drugs. After the pure heroin is purchased in large quantities, it is subsequently diluted and resold. By the time it is finally used by an addict, the substance has been diluted many times and its price has increased perhaps thirty times the original cost. 

Other opium derivatives, such as codeine, dilaudid, methadone, and demerol, have the same sleep-producing and painrelieving factors as morphine and heroin. Tolerance, or the capacity to endure the effects of drugs, is very highly developed during addiction to the opiates. The user becomes physically dependent upon these drugs and begins to feel uncomfortable within a few hours after the last dose has been administered.

The opiates tend to relieve psychic tension and to produce a pleasantly relaxed and dreamy state.

Addiction to sedatives and to hypnotics is very similar to chronic alcoholism. Addiction to barbiturates is very common and steadily increasing. Simultaneous use of barbiturates and of alcohol is quite common, and the signs of intoxication with any of these drugs are similar to those with alcohol. They include mental sluggishness, emotional instability, confusion, and slurring of speech.

Marihuana is smoked in the form of cigarettes commonly called "reefers." The marihuana cigarette contains the shredded leaves of the female Cannabis Sativa plant, and the active ingredient is transmitted in some degree to the smoke of the burning plant fragments in the cigarette. Marihuana is not considered an addicting drug in that it does not create a physical dependence and severe withdrawal symptoms. It is, however, a habit-forming drug and creates a psychological dependence in most users. It is a conditioner for the use of the more dangerously addicting drugs of heroin and other opiate derivatives. The marihuana smoker is usually associated with a peddler of narcotics who is sure to attempt to persuade the smoker to try heroin or perhaps other drugs for a bigger thrill. Many marihuana smokers and drug addicts become addicted when they try drugs out of curiosity or simply for the thrill of it. Because a "reefer" can be bought from street peddlers, this type of "addiction" has extended to teen-agers. The greatest danger in the use of marihuana is that many users turn eventually to heroin addiction. 

The effects of marihuana on the user are sometimes unpredictable. Generally speaking, it has much the same effect as the excessive use of alcohol in that it releases inhibitions. The initial effect of marihuana is a mild intoxication in which everything seems pleasant to the user and he has a strong sense of wellbeing. After that, the auditory sense is affected and the user ordinarily suffers from mild hallucinations. He loses his ability to judge distances. He then progresses into a stage of ecstatic dreams or nightmares according to the mood in which he happens to be. His self-control is weakened and his imagination becomes riotous. In this state, the user may become dangerous and belligerent and may develop homicidal or suicidal tendencies. 

The final state is one of extreme depression followed by deep sleep. Ordinarily, users of depressant narcotics are not violent while under the influence of the drug. When marihuana is combined with alcohol, however, the user becomes insensitive to pain and violent encounters often occur as a result. 

The withdrawal symptoms of marihuana develop when the addict is deprived of the drug. He is tense, restless, irritable,

and depressed. He suffers severe headaches and abdominal cramps. A confirmed addict devolops a yellowish pallor of the skin especially around the eyes which seem to be distended, presenting a pop-eyed appearance.

ARGOT OF DRUG USERS 

Drug users have a vocabulary all their own. A few of the more common terms used by addicts are included here because the law enforcement officer, when dealing with drug addiction, comes in contact with many of them. While being questioned by officers about narcotics addiction, the drug addict invariably uses a number of these terms, and it is difficult to follow him unless the officers are familiar with the vocabulary. The most common terms are the following: "Tea"-marihuana. "Cap"-one-grain capsule of narcotics. "Reefer"-marihuana cigarette. "Cold turkey" or "kicking the habit"-trying to break the drug habit. "Fix" or "shot"-an injection of narcotics. "Shooting Gallery"-a place where narcotics can be purchased, or where drug injections are administered. "Hopped-up"-under the influence of marihuana. "Hooked"-a stage following physical dependence, addiction. "Joy-popper”-a person who is not a confirmed addict, but who occasionally takes an injection or uses marihuana. "Junkie”—a pronounced daily dosage; a confirmed addict. "Main-liner"—a main vein or intravenous injection. "Pusher"-a peddler of narcotics. "Goof-balls," "goofers," "red birds," "yellow jackets," "rainbows," and "green dragons"-various brands of barbiturates usually designated by the color of the different brands.

The terms, habituation, tolerance, and addiction, are often used in connection with the use of narcotics. These terms are not necessarily a part of the addict's special vocabulary, but are used to describe the various stages of drug addiction. Habituation is the tendency to become mentally dependent upon a drug. 

It is the stage in which a person using drugs thinks he cannot get along without it, but from a physical standpoint, no symptoms or ill effects result when he is deprived of it. Tolerance develops in a drug user when larger and larger quantities of narcotics must be taken to produce the desired mental or physical effects. A person develops drug addiction when his body has become so dependent upon the drug that he suffers greatly

and symptoms develop when it is withdrawn. Addiction is a more advanced stage than mere habituation. When addiction is severe, sudden withdrawal of the drugs may result in death.

Withdrawal is another term used to describe the physical and mental effects on an addict when he is denied the drugs he has been using habitually. "Withdrawal symptoms," which are discussed later in this chapter, show up usually when an addict is taking a cure for drug addiction or is imprisoned and unable to obtain narcotics.

RELATIONSHIP OF DRUG ADDICTION TO CRIME 

There is a definite relationship between drug addiction and crime. Drug addiction is probably the most expensive habit known to man and one of the most difficult to break. Hence, it is easily understandable why addicts, when their funds are exhausted, resort to crime to procure money for the purchase of drugs. On the average, a "hooked" addict spends from $10.00 to $20.00 daily in order to maintain the habit. 

Generally speaking, addicts are not dangerous while under the influence of drugs, with the exception of marihuana users and possibly others. The dangerous addict is one with a craving for the drug and unable to obtain it because he has no money. 

When under the influence of heroin, for example, the addict is not particularly dangerous. Addicts in this condition are in a state of pleasure. The dangerous addict is one seeking a way to obtain narcotics. It is the constant demand for money to procure drugs that causes the addict to steal and to rob, and to commit crimes of assault and those of a more serious nature. The addict will go to almost any extreme to avoid being arrested because he knows he will be deprived of drugs if placed in jail.

As a result of addiction, the addict loses all interest in his work and is unable to hold a job for any length of time. When his physical and mental abilities have been retarded or destroyed by drugs, the addict turns readily to crime. The male addict often begins with crimes of shoplifting, forgery, petty thievery, and procuring. The female addict resorts generally to prostitution, her quickest way of obtaining money. 

EFFECTS OF DRUG ADDICTION ON THE USER 

Physical deterioration is brought about in an individual through the excessive use of drugs. The vital secretions of the body are decreased through steady use of drugs, and the mouth becomes acid. Teeth rot and fall out. With secretions decreased in the stomach and in the intestines, the addict loses his natural appetite for food and becomes sluggish. The addict suffers from

loss of energy and constipation or diarrhea, depending upon the drug. The eyes and skin turn yellow, and the gall bladder becomes inflamed. If an addict "sniffs" narcotic drugs, the membranous lining of the nose becomes inflamed and red. Ulcers appear in the nostrils, and breathing is slowed down considerably as a result of the poisonous action of overdosages of drugs. The addict's inability to breathe normally reduces the supply of oxygen needed for his blood. Over a period of time, the addict becomes easy prey to such diseases as tuberculosis. In advanced stages, drug addicts often suffer severe pains, their veins gradually collapse, and the skin is left with a purple discoloration. 

Boils and absesses are the result of injuries inflicted on the skin through septic hypodermic injections.

Addiction to narcotic drugs causes not only physical deterioration but also mental and emotional damage. Strength of character is weakened greatly, and the victim becomes mentally sluggish and lives more or less in a state of stupor. Addicts are careless about their personal appearance. Through loss of interest, addicts find it difficult to hold a steady job, and are indifferent toward society in general. The one interest of the dope addict lies in acquiring money to maintain the drug habit. 

A continuous use of habit-forming drugs often leads to insanity or to a career of crime or both. Drug addiction wrecks the nervous system. The repeated use of drugs weakens a person's natural resistance to diseases that result in death. An addict's life span may be twenty to twenty-five years less than that of a normal person.

Morphine and the opiates are thrown off by the body through the intestines and kidneys resulting in body poisoning. Retention of these poisons eventually has a deleterious effect on the sex organs. Sterility results, and women who are able to bear children often pass drug poisoning along to their offspring. The effect of drug addiction upon sexual behavior differs according to the type of drug used. The opiate drugs tend to make the reproductive system inactive. The sexual secretions diminish, and the general tendency is to reduce or to obliterate sexual desires. 

Opiate addiction becomes more a substitute for sex rather than a stimulation of sexual activity. Homosexual tendencies may develop from the use of drugs.

On the other hand, the marihuana drug may have the opposite effect from opiate drugs on sexual behavior. Marihuana tends to stimulate sexual desires because it releases inhibitions and is characterized by a complete lack of moral restraint. At gatherings of marihuana smokers, where addicts may smoke as many as ten or twelve reefers, sexual promiscuity often occurs.

TAKING THE CURE - THE "WITHDRAWAL PERIOD" 

Once a person has become addicted to narcotic drugs, it is difficult to withdraw from the habit. Under the present circumstances surrounding drug addiction, cures are comparatively rare. "Withdrawal symptoms" vary usually according to the type of drugs used. Generally, however, during the withdrawal period the addict becomes uncomfortable and somewhat nervous six to eight hours after the last injection of an opiate drug has been administered. The usual pattern of opiate withdrawal symptoms begins twelve to fourteen hours after the last dose. The person becomes drowsy or falls into restless sleep. About twentyfour hours after the drug has been discontinued, the addict suffers a watery nasal discharge, sweating, frequent yawning, dilation of the pupils of the eyes, and waves of goose flesh. The symptoms become more severe as time passes. Restlessness is intensified and the addict becomes unable to eat. He vomits frequently and has diarrhea or constipation. He experiences severe twitching of all the muscles of the body. Respiration increases and a slight fever develops. Symptoms reach their peak some forty-eight hours after the last dose has been administered and continue usually for seventy-two hours. Seven to ten days afterwards, most of the objective symptoms of withdrawal disappear; however, the addict is still nervous and weak. 

Rehabilitation services are available to drug addicts and are being used at the United States Public Health Service hospitals at Lexington, Kentucky, and at Fort Worth, Texas, as well as at various state and private institutions. In the State of New York, for instance, a hospital has been set up for youthful addicts. 

FEDERAL NARCOTICS LAWS 

The first regulatory federal narcotics measure in the United States was the Act of 1909 which prohibited the importation and use of opium for other than medicinal purposes. This was followed in 1914 by the Harrison Narcotic Act which was a taxing measure designed to have the effect of regulating the domestic trade and distribution of narcotic drugs.

The Act of 1909 was consolidated and amplified, and given the title of the Narcotic Drug Import and Export Act, in 1922. 

It was further amended in 1924 to limit generally, the importation and exportation of narcotic drugs, to prohibit the importation of opium for the manufacture of heroin, and, as a practical measure, to outlaw heroin altogether in the United States. 

Although the Harrison Narcotic Law was enacted in the form of a revenue measure, it also served to give effect to certain international regulations of opium. The Opium Poppy Con-

trol Act of 1942 was based on treaty obligations pursuant to international control of opium use, and it prohibited the growing of the opium poppy in the United States. 

The Marihuana Tax Act of 1937 limited the use of marihuana to legitimate medical needs. It has since been abandoned as having no acceptable medical use. Hence, marihuana is now on the same basis as heroin. Both drugs are illegal. Public Law 255, 82nd Congress, known as the Boggs Act, amended the penalty provisions of the Harrison Narcotic Law, the Narcotic Drugs 

Import and Export Act, and the Marihuana Tax Act, to provide minimum penalties of two years for first offenders, five years for second offenders, and ten years for third offenders. Second or subsequent offenders are not entitled to probationary or suspended sentences.

By a recent amendment to the Internal Revenue narcotics laws (Public Law 729, 83rd Congress, 1954), authority was granted whereby registered retail dealers and druggists, subject to stated conditions, might fill oral prescriptions for certain narcotic drugs. and compounds which were found to possess relatively little or no addiction liability.

Federal narcotics laws are enforced by the Treasury Department. In 1930, the Bureau of Narcotics was set up in the Treasury Department which is charged with the investigation, detection, and prevention of violations of the federal narcotics, marihuana, and opium control acts. These laws are enforced by approximately 250 agents of the Bureau of Narcotics.

All states have some laws regulating the possession, sale, and use of narcotics. State and local law enforcement officers usually work closely with federal officers in the suppression of the illicit sale and use of narcotics.

TEXAS UNIFORM NARCOTIC DRUG ACT Art. 725b P.C. Narcotic Drug Regulation. 

Under Texas law, "narcotic drugs" means coca leaves, opium and canabis, amidone, and isonipecaine, and their derivatives, and the addicting drugs designated by the federal narcotics laws. (Sec. 1.)

It is unlawful for any person to manufacture, possess, have under his control, sell, prescribe, administer, dispense, or compound any narcotic drug (Sec. 2), except where authorized. (Sec. 2A.) Duly-registered wholesalers and manufacturers may sell and dispense narcotic drugs on written order to apothecaries, physicians, dentists, veterinarians, or persons in charge of hospitals or laboratories or other persons especially designated in Section 5. Such persons may in turn use the drugs in their official capacities as set out in Sections 6-10.

20 Any place which is resorted to by drug addicts for the purpose of using narcotic drugs, or any place which illegally keeps or sells drugs, is deemed common nuisance. (Sec. 13.) All narcotic drugs illegally possessed are contraband, and are subject to seizure by any employee of the Department of Public Safety or by any peace officer authorized to enforce the provisions of this 

Act. (Secs. 14 and 22.) When such officers have reason to believe there has been a violation of this Act, they have authority to search without warrant, any structures, places or conveyances, except those used as private residences, in which event a search warrant is required. Any officer seizing narcotics must issue a receipt for the drugs to the person from whom they are taken, and then file immediately a sworn inventory of these drugs with any magistrate in the county where the drugs are taken. (Secs. 

15 and 16.) 

Sec. 23 provides that any person violating any provision of this Act shall, upon conviction, be punished by confinement in the state penitentiary for not less than two years nor more than life, and upon the second or any subsequent conviction therefor, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for life or for any term of years not less than ten, and the benefits of the suspended sentence law shall not be available to a defendant convicted for a violation of the provisions of this Act; provided that any person convicted for a first offense violation shall be entitled to the benefits of probation under the Adult Probation and Parole Law. 

Any adult person who uses a minor under nineteen years of age in unlawfully transporting, peddling, etc., narcotic drugs, or who gives or sells, etc., narcotics to a minor under nineteen, shall be liable to a sentence of not less than five years nor more than life, and upon the second conviction shall be punished for life or for any term of years not less than ten. The benefits of the suspended sentence law shall not be available to a defendant convicted for a violation of this Act. 

Art. 725b (2A) P.C. Narcotic Paraphernalia. 

It is unlawful for any person to possess paraphernalia, hypodermic syringe, needle, etc., which is used for administering narcotic drugs, except under the direction of a licensed physician. 

Art. 725c. Compulsory Treatment of Narcotic Addicts. 

It shall be unlawful for any person to habitually use narcotic drugs, be addicted to the use of narcotic drugs, or be under the influence of narcotic drugs, provided, however, that nothing in this section shall be applicable to a person who has a medical need for narcotic drugs and who obtains the narcotic drugs re-

quired for such medical need in accordance with the laws of the 

State of Texas and of the United States. (Sec. 2.) 

Any person violating any provision of this Act shall be guilty of a felony, and upon conviction shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for a period of not more than three years. The benefits of the suspended sentence law shall not be available to a person convicted of violating any provision of this Act. (Sec. 3.)

Laws relating to adult probation are applicable to cases where a defendant has been convicted or has entered a plea of guilty to a violation of this Act, except that in cases charging a violation of this Act, probation may be granted notwithstanding that the defendant may have previously been convicted of a felony. The court may include among the conditions of the probation that the probationer shall enter a hospital approved by the court and remain there until discharged by the medical authorities of such hospital as cured. (Sec. 4.)

Art. 725d P.C. Transportation or Possession of Contraband Narcotics- 

Seizure and Forfeiture.

It is unlawful to transport, carry or convey, conceal or possess any contraband narcotics in, upon or by means of any vessel, vehicle or aircraft or any occupants thereof; or to use any vessel, vehicle or aircraft or occupants thereof to facilitate the transportation, carriage, conveyance, concealment, receipt, possession, purchase, sale, barter, exchange or gift of any contraband narcotics. (Sec. 1.) Any vessel, vehicle or aircraft which is being used in violation of this Act is liable to seizure and forfeiture. (Sec. 2.)

Art. 726b P.C. provides for the regulation of the sale of barbiturates. Art. 726c provides penalties for the unlawful sale and distribution of barbiturates. The fine for violation of this Act is $3,000 and the penitentiary sentence is ten years, with benefits of suspended sentence denied to second or subsequent offenders.

CHAPTER Rules of Evidence in Texas Courts DEFINITION 

Evidence is the system of rules and standards by which the admission of proof at the trial of a lawsuit is regulated. Evidence is primarily a rule of exclusion, that is, what evidence is not admissible in court. Any species of proof, or probative matter, legally presented at the trial of an issue, by the act of the parties, and through the medium of witnesses, records, documents, concrete objects, etc., for the purpose of inducing belief in the minds of the court and the jury as to their contention, are properly called evidence.

As a part of procedure, evidence signifies those rules of law whereby it is determined what testimony should be admitted and what should be rejected in each case, and what is the weight to be given to the testimony admitted. Exhibits, facts admitted, facts judicially noticed, inferences, medical testimony, etc., are admissible as evidence.

DIRECT EVIDENCE 

Direct evidence is proof of the facts by the witnesses who saw the acts done or heard the words spoken. Direct testimony of witnesses as to the existence or nonexistence of the thing in issue or the presentation of the object itself for observation by the court and jury, is direct evidence. For example, on general principles, it would seem that when the question is whether a certain object is black or white, the best evidence would be the exhibition of the object itself. 

In criminal cases, this principle permits the introduction of any article, object or substance which is shown to have been a part or an incident of the criminal offense charged, or means of committing it. Examples of objects admitted into evidence are: articles on which fingerprints have been found, a watch found on the body of the dead man, bottle claimed to have contained poison by which victim died, bones claimed to be those of a murder victim, teeth, buttons, etc., which tend to identify a victim who was burned; and in theft prosecutions, the stolen articles found in defendant's possession.

When the accused is on trial for homicide or assault, the weapon by which the crime was committed, such as a pistol, knife, ax, etc., may be introduced into evidence by the state. 

Articles of wearing apparel belonging to the victim or to the ac-

cused, are admissible in criminal cases when they tend to throw some light on a particular issue in the case, such as, that the victim died as a result of violence. All objects submitted into evidence must be offered for a specific purpose, that is, to prove some specific contention.

CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE 

Circumstantial evidence includes all evidence of an indirect nature and is often as strong and conclusive as direct and positive evidence. A case founded solely upon circumstantial evidence arises when there are no eyewitnesses to the crime. If direct testimony is available, there is no need for circumstantial evidence. Circumstantial evidence is the proof of collateral facts and circumstances from which the mind arrives at the conclusion that the main facts sought to be established, in fact existed. Circumstantial evidence, namely, collateral facts, may become important when the defendant in a homicide case pleads self-defense, and evidence of his state of mind is offered to show whether he reasonably apprehended an attack by the deceased. 

Where there is no direct evidence to show who was the aggressor, the deceased's character for violence has probative value. In a rape case, the prosecutrix' character for unchastity, is relevant for the purpose of showing that she probably consented to the intercourse.

When a person is charged with a certain act, his physical or mental capacity for doing the act may usually be used in aid of more direct evidence leading to the inference that he did the act. Conversely, his incapacity evidences the improbability that he did the act. The habit or custom of a person can be significant circumstantial evidence. In a direct evidence case of murder, the motive is not important; however, in a circumstantial evidence case, the element of motive does become an essential element. Motive shows probability.

Evidence is offered by the state in order to prove the guilt of the accused beyond a reasonable doubt. Circumstantial evidence may meet the "reasonable doubt" requirement. Introduction by the defense of an "alibi" to a charge, is an obvious example of evidence which may cast a reasonable doubt as to his guilt. Evidence of suicide in a homicide prosecution is another example.

A "consciousness of guilt" on the part of the accused, may be received as evidence tending to show that he committed the crime with which he is charged. This might include flight from justice, resistance of arrest, escape from custody, etc. The ac-

cused, of course, may always offer any facts in explanation of his conduct.

Where there have been prior allegations of crime, either charges or convictions, against the accused, it is admissible to offer evidence showing a common scheme or plan between the prior offenses and the present crime for which the accused is being prosecuted, such as similar disguises and tools used in robberies. The state may offer evidence to show the absence of mistake or accident in a prosecution for murder by poison, such as where several members of the accused's family had mysteriously died from poisoning.

ADMISSIBILITY 

Admissible evidence is that which is of such character that the court or judge is bound to accept it, that is, allow it to be introduced. In the trial of a case, evidence must be offered according to set rules; otherwise, it is subject to objection and will be excluded as irrelevant, immaterial or incompetent. Evidence may be admissible for one purpose and inadmissible for another; hence, it must be offered for the proper purpose. Evidence is relevant when it applies to the matter in question affording something to the purpose. Material evidence is that which is important, or more or less necessary, having influence or effect, as distinguished from being merely relevant or bearing upon the issue. Competent evidence is that which is duly qualified and answers all the requirements of the rules, having sufficient ability or authority and possessing the legal qualifications of evidence. A witness is said to be competent when he is legally fit and qualified to give testimony in court. 

The law enforcement officer should have a basic knowledge of the law of evidence and the rules which govern its admissibility in the trial of a case, so that he will be better able to gather and to sort the facts which come to his attention. All facts surrounding the commission of a crime or accident, are possible sources of evidence; hence, the officer should overlook nothing. The gathering of pertinent facts as evidence is a skill which comes from experience once the fundamentals are learned. 

WHO MAY TESTIFY IN COURT 

According to Art. 708 C.C.P., all persons are competent to testify in criminal cases except the following: 

1. Persons who are insane at the time of the trial or who were in that condition when the event happened of which they are called to testify.

25 2. Children or persons who do not appear to possess sufficient intellect to relate transactions with respect to which they are interrogated, or who do not understand the obligation of an oath. 

3. All persons who have been or may be convicted of a felony in the state, and who are confined to the penitentiary or in jail, shall be permitted to testify in person, in court, as to any offense committed on a state prison farm, or state prison, or committed on a railroad train or public highway along which prisoners are being transported under guard, or is committed in some jail.

Art. 709 C.C.P. Female Alleged to Be Seduced. 

In prosecutions for seduction, the female shall be permitted to testify; but no conviction shall be had upon her testimony alone unless the same is corroborated by other evidence tending to connect the defendant with the offense charged. 

Art. 710 C.C.P. Defendant May Testify. 

Any defendant in a criminal action will be permitted to testify in his own behalf therein, but the failure of any defendant to so testify shall not be taken as a circumstance against him, nor shall the same be alluded to or commented on by counsel in the cause; provided that where there are two or more persons jointly charged or indicted, and a severance is had, the privilege of testifying shall be extended only to the party on trial.

Art. 711 C.C.P. Principals, Accomplices or Accessories. 

Persons charged as principals, accomplices or accessories, whether in the same or in different indictments, cannot be introduced as witnesses for another, but they may claim a severance; and, if any one or more be acquitted, or the prosecution against them be dismissed, they may testify in behalf of the other.

Art. 718 C.C.P. Testimony of Accomplice. 

A conviction cannot be had upon the testimony of an accomplice unless corroborated by other evidence tending to connect the defendant with the offense committed; and the corroboration is not sufficient if it merely shows the commission of the offense.

However, the uncorroborated testimony of an accomplice is sufficient to sustain a conviction under special provisions in the following articles of the Penal Code: Art. 607-21, 642c, 652, 652a, 

666-23a, 666-28. When an accomplice is required to testify concerning a prohibited transaction, he is exempt from prosecution. 

The female subject of an abortion is neither a principal nor an

accomplice, and may testify against persons performing or conspiring to perform an abortion.

Art. 712 C.C.P. Court May Determine Competency. 

The court may, upon suggestion made, or of its own option, interrogate a person who is offered as a witness, to ascertain whether he is competent to testify, or if the competency or incompetency of the witness may be shown by evidence. 

Art. 715 C.C.P. Religious Opinion. 

No person is incompetent to testify on account of his religious opinion or for the want of any religious belief. 

Art. 716 C.C.P. Defendant Jointly Indicted. 

A defendant jointly indicted with others, and who has been tried and convicted, and whose punishment was fine only, may testify for the other defendant after he has paid the fine and costs.

THE BEST EVIDENCE RULE 

The Best Evidence Rule is used to prove-up the authenticity of a document or any written instrument. Where inquiry is being made as to the contents of a material document, the only competent evidence of such contents is the document itself, unless the document is first shown to be unavailable as being lost or destroyed, or absence from the jurisdiction of the court without fault on the part of the person offering the evidence, or as being in the possession of the adversary where he has been notified to produce it, or as being a part of the public records, in all of which cases other evidence may be resorted to. 

The rule requiring the production of the original writing applies only where the purpose of the evidence is to prove the contents of the document. The best evidence is the original document. In the absence of the original, a duplication in some form is the best evidence. In the absence of an original or copy, memory may be the best evidence. In all cases the absence of the original must be accounted for. The original is always that which was intended to be the original; hence, even a carbon copy may be an "original" if it were so intended. 

Art. 3737e R.C.S. Business Records as Evidence. 

The crux of this article is that business records and reports may be offered into evidence. A memo or record insofar as it is relevant, shall be competent evidence if the judge finds that: 

1. It was made in the regular course of business. 

2. It was the regular course of business for an employee or representative, with personal knowledge of the act, con-

dition or event, to make such memo or to transmit information to be included in the memo or record. 

3. It was made at or near the time of the act, event or condition or reasonably soon afterwards.

Identity and mode of preparation of the memo or record may be proved by testimony of the entrant, or other qualified witness although he has no personal knowledge of the contents. 

Such lack of knowledge may affect weight and credibility of the memo or record but shall not affect its admissibility. 

Evidence to the effect that records of a business do not contain any memo or record of an alleged act, shall be competent to prove the nonoccurrence of the act or condition in that business, if it were customary in that business to make a memo or record of all such acts at that time or within reasonable time thereafter to preserve them. "Business" includes any and every kind of regular, organized activity whether conducted for a profit or not. 

An example of the use of Art. 3737e might arise where a police officer who made out an accident report is dead at the time of trial, but his accident report is an "official" record of the department; then, such record may be placed in evidence. 

Art. 3726 R.C.S. Official Records as Evidence. 

The previous article, 3737e, refers to business records which are not required by law to be kept. Art. 3726 refers to official recorded documents required to be kept by law, such as marriage licenses, deed records, state hospital reports, birth records, etc. 

These may be introduced into evidence by citing the proper location of records or documents, in the official records. 

EXPERT TESTIMONY AND OPINION 

In the trial of any case a witness ordinarily testifies of facts which he has observed firsthand. Opinions are usually inadmissible. An opinion, however, will be received into evidence when it is an aid to the jury, and if it is no help to the jury it is immaterial and superfluous. The admission of opinions depends upon the competency of the witness and the relevancy of his testimony. An opinion is evidence of what the witness thinks, believes, or infers with regard to facts in dispute, as distinguished from his personal knowledge of the facts themselves. 

A witness who has been shown to be learned, skilled or experienced in a specific field is called an expert witness. Experts are often called upon to give opinions. A lay witness may give an opinion under certain conditions, but only on facts which he has observed. An expert may give an opinion not only on facts which he has observed, but also on facts not observed; an opinion

on unobserved facts is given in answer to a hypothetical question. 

All witnesses, whether lay or expert, must be properly qualified before they are permitted to give an opinion of any kind. The court decides whether or not a witness is an expert. The jury also weighs the testimony of the expert and may accept his opinion or reject it. A witness is an expert witness and is qualified to give expert testimony if the judge finds that to perceive, to know or to understand the matter concerning which the witness is to testify, requires special knowledge, skill, experience or training, and that the witness has the requisite special knowledge, skill or training. A physician, for example, is generally considered to be a medical expert in most all fields of medicine.

Opinion testimony is not restricted to experts. Lay witnesses may give opinions as to the results of their observations, made at the time, in regard to common appearances or facts, and the condition of things which cannot be adequately described verbally for a jury merely by giving facts observed. Such evidence is competent from a matter of necessity on the same grounds as the testimony of experts, as the only method of proving certain facts essential to the proper administration of justice. This is often called a "shorthand rendering of the facts." 

A lay witness, if qualified and competent, may testify and give opinions on matters of sanity, sobriety, drunkenness, emotional states, speed of vehicles, etc. In many cases, it is not possible for the ordinary witness to give such facts as will convey to the jury the exact impression which was made upon the witness. Hence, a qualified witness might say: "The man acted crazy," or "he looked sober," or "he was drunk," or "he was emotionally upset and nervous," or "in my opinion, the automobile was traveling over thirty miles per hour."

Opinion testimony is received into evidence only on matters which are proper subjects of opinion testimony. No witness is permitted to give his opinion directly that a person is guilty or innocent, or is criminally responsible, or that a person was negligent or careful or careless, etc. The reason for this is that these are questions of law, and no witness is recognized as an expert on law; obviously, it is the purpose of the trial to determine such questions from the facts involved. 

Art. 731 C.C.P. Evidence of Handwriting. 

It is competent to give evidence of handwriting by comparison, made by experts or by the jury. Proof by comparison only shall not be sufficient, however, to establish the handwriting of a witness who denies his signature under oath. For example, in a forgery case, one or more experts may examine a signature

29 on an allegedly forged instrument, and the handwriting of a signature on another instrument allegedly written by the accused. The experts may give their opinions on the comparison of the handwriting. If the defendant denies under oath, that he wrote the particular instrument used as the object of comparison, other corroborative evidence is necessary to establish proof. (176 S.W. 2d 327.) Proof by comparison only is insufficient for a conviction when a denial under oath is made. Under Art. 731, it is probably admissible to make a comparison of typewriters. 

Any person familiar with the handwriting of another may testify as to the genuineness of a disputed writing claimed to be that of such other person. The witness need not be an expert on handwriting, but he must be familiar with regard to the particular signature in question. This may be done by showing that the witness has seen the person write, has frequently inspected papers containing his handwriting, etc. A similar rule permits comparison of handwriting in civil cases under Art. 

3737b R.C.S.

HEARSAY 

Hearsay is evidence which does not proceed from the personal knowledge of the witness, but from the mere repetition of what he has heard others say. Hearsay is simply an out-ofcourt statement. The truth of the statement does not rest upon the credit of the witness on the stand, but upon the veracity and competency of other persons who are not present in court, and who are not subject to cross-examination. Hearsay is secondhand evidence; it is what the witness said he heard someone else say. Such evidence is inadmissible because made out of court, not under oath, and not subject to the test of crossexamination.

Hearsay evidence is incompetent and is subject to an objection unless some special exception makes it competent. In the absence of such objection, pure hearsay evidence alone will not support a verdict. Examples of pure hearsay are: newspaper accounts, or evidence of statements to a witness by someone over the telephone. It is hearsay for the witness to state that he heard the murder victim speak of being threatened. 

A "verbal act" is not considered hearsay, that is, where an act about which the witness is giving evidence is so tied up with the words which were spoken that both are necessary to convey the information, then it is permissible to tell what the witness heard also. This is similar to the res gestae exception which is discussed later in this chapter.

EXCEPTIONS TO THE HEARSAY RULE 

More important, practically speaking, than the hearsay rule itself, are the exceptions to the rule. Under certain well known and accepted situations, hearsay evidence is admissible in the trial of a case. The more important exceptions are the following. 

REFRESHING A PRESENT AND PAST RECOLLECTION 

It often happens that a case comes up for trial several months after the event in question has occurred, and where the memory of the witness has become dim. Oftentimes, the witness has simply forgotten certain facts which previously he had observed.
