It is possible to refresh the memory of a witness through the use of notes or memos, etc. The witness may look over the notes or memos and if they refresh his memory, he may then testify; not by reading the notes, but by testifying from his memory. This is called "refreshing a present recollection," and is not, strictly speaking, hearsay, because the witness is still testifying from his memory.

On the other hand, even with the use of notes and memos, the memory of the witness may not be refreshed; in other words, after looking at the notes, the witness still may fail to remember what he observed. In such a case, a memo may be offered as recorded evidence of the witness' memory which has now vanished. Then, it is the memo and not the memory, which constitutes the evidence. Such evidence of out-of-court statements, is hearsay, but is admissible as an exception to the rule. 

The memo, in order to be introduced into evidence, must meet the following requirements: 

1. The memo must have been made at or near the time of the event, that is, contemporaneously with the transaction in question. This is somewhat indefinite; however, a satisfactory rule is that the facts must have been fairly fresh in the mind of the witness when they were recorded. An example of this might be an autopsy report written while it was being performed or shortly thereafter, or an accident report written the following day from notes taken at the scene of the accident. 

2. The witness must guarantee the correctness of the memorandum. The memo must accurately reflect the recollection of the witness, and therefore the witness must vouch for the validity and authenticity of the memo. He must remember when he made out the memo, or saw it being made out, and that he knew it to be correct when written.

3. The original or a copy of the memo must actually be produced.

The memo becomes part of the evidence. When the witness identifies the memo and guarantees its correctness, the writing as well as his testimony are admissible into evidence. The two taken together constitute his present positive statement, affirming the truth of the contents of the memo. The foregoing requirements are not necessary where notes and memos are merely to refresh a present, as distinguished from a past, recollection.

THE ANCIENT DOCUMENT RULE 

Where an instrument is at least thirty years old and is shown to have come from the proper custody and is free from suspicion with no obvious alterations on the face of the document, it is admissible into evidence without direct proof of its execution. (120 S.W. 228.)

DECLARATIONS OF BODILY CONDITION OF DECLARANT 

Where bodily conditions are in question, it is permissible for a witness to testify as to what he heard out of court. For example, the witness might say that he heard X state, "My head hurts." In the absence of proof that X made such a statement or similar statements about his bodily condition in order to create favorable evidence for himself, a witness may testify to what he heard, although this is pure hearsay.

Evidence of bodily condition is used most often where a physician testifies as to what he was told by a patient regarding bodily conditions, pain, sensation, etc. Where a person consults a physician for the purpose of securing the testimony of the physician in court, then, the medical witness is limited in his testimony to those objective facts which he has observed through medical examination, such as cuts, bruises, swelling, fever, etc.

An ordinary witness, not a physician, may also testify as to obvious conditions, such as cuts and bruises, etc. On the other hand, statements which a patient makes to his physician concerning subjective symptoms, something which cannot be determined by an ordinary examination, are admissible into evidence provided the patient has consulted the physician for treatment rather than merely to obtain his testimony in court. In no case may the physician relate the history of the case, that is, what the patient said in describing how he sustained his injuries.

22 

 DECLARATIONS AS TO PRESENT STATE OF MIND OR PRESENT INTENT 

Hearsay evidence of a person's intention to do or not to do an act is admissible to show that this act was probably done or not done. Where the only evidence available to prove the point in question, is the present intention which was expressed, it is admissible for what it is worth to the jury. A prime example of present intent is a threat made prior to the commission of a crime. Evidence that the threat was made indicates that the crime was committed accordingly. Before such declarations of intent are admissible it must be shown that they were made under circumstances which would usually and normally be made, and where there is an absence of probability that the declaration was made for the purpose of manufacturing evidence. 

Declarations of intention, plan or design in criminal cases are usually in the form of threats of violence. Evidence of threats is admissible to show ill will was present when the crime was committed.

According to Art. 1258 P.C., where a defendant accused of murder seeks to justify himself on the ground of threats against his own life, he may be permitted to introduce evidence of the threats made, but the same shall not be regarded as affording justification for the offense unless it be shown that at the time of the homicide the person killed by some act done, manifested an intention to execute the threat so made. Threats which were made to the accused can only be brought into a self-defense case. 

These threats are admissible for the purpose of showing a reasonable apprehension of danger on the part of the accused irrespective of their truth, so long as the defendant believed that he had a reasonable apprehension of danger. It is immaterial who relayed the threats to the defendant. Where the defendant has not heard the threat, or heard of the threat from someone else, that is, where the threat is uncommunicated to the defendant, he may not offer evidence of the threat to show that he was acting under a reasonable apprehension of danger. 

Obviously, he would not apprehend danger where he had not heard of any threat against him. On the other hand, evidence of threats whether the defendant heard them or not, is admissible to show who was the probable aggressor, or who started the argument for example, which resulted in the homicide. In brief, the rule is, in a self-defense case, that communicated threats may be offered to show that the defendant acted in selfdefense; uncommunicated threats may be offered to show that the deceased was the probable aggressor. Here is an example of evidence which is admissible for one purpose but inadmissible

for some other purpose. The evidence must always be offered for the proper reason.

SPONTANEOUS UTTERANCES -RES GESTAE 

Anything that is said at the time of the commission of an act or just prior thereto, or immediately thereafter, is admissible into evidence under the rule of res gestae. This exception to the hearsay rule is permitted in order that bare facts may be used to fill in the narrative and to tell the complete story. It is accepted because the statements involved are made in the excitement of an incident where there is little opportunity for reflection and calculated deception, and therefore, free from bias. A res gestae statement simply is one made spontaneously and without deliberation. The Texas courts are rather lenient in the matter of res gestae statements, and have wide discretion in admitting such evidence. Declarations by persons injured in accidents, and those made by victims of crimes of violence, are the usual types of res gestae statements. Under the stress of the situation, it is presumed that the truth will come out by virtue of the spontaneity of the statement. The test is the impulsiveness of the circumstances.

It is also possible for a mere bystander to make a res gestae statement which would be admissible into evidence so long as he makes the statement at the very time he receives the impression which prompts the statement. For example, where it was an issue in the case as to whether a train stopped at a certain crossing, the remark of a bystander that the train entered the crossing without stopping, made when the bystander was observing the train, was admissible. (41 S.W. 167.) Statements made by the victim a short time after a fatal shooting as to how it occurred are admissible under the res gestae rule. (51 S.W. 2d 385.)

In rape cases, complaints by the prosecutrix are generally admissible under the res gestae rule. Examples are: An elevenyear-old child made statements one-half hour after the alleged attack, while the victim was still suffering and excited, and her statements were held admissible as voluntary and spontaneous. (19 S.W. 892.) The following statements by the prosecutrix to her mother were admitted when she returned home in an excited condition after the attack: "He raped me, he raped me; he done it to me twice," etc., with further details. (287 S.W. 269.)

RES GESTAE OF THE ARREST 

An interesting corollary to the res gestae exception is called the "res gestae of the arrest" which applies in criminal cases.

Art. 727 C.C.P. requires that all statements of a person under arrest, in order to be admissible against him in a trial, must be in writing and signed by the accused; however, the "res gestae of the arrest" theory permits the admission of spontaneous declarations, made by the accused at the time of the arrest although they have not been put in writing. An example of this would be: The accused was arrested on fresh pursuit within five minutes after he committed a homicide, and he said, upon being accused of murder by the party making the arrest, "If I have killed him, nobody saw me," and this statement was admitted into evidence. (5 S.W. 153.) Where a defendant was arrested in the act of unlawfully transporting liquor, he made a statement to the sheriff making the arrest that he was taking it home for medicinal purposes, and the statement was admitted into evidence. (276 S.W. 928.) Res gestae evidence was held admissible in favor of a defendant when, at the time of the arrest for unlawfully transporting liquor, he stated that he had found the bottles and did not know what they contained. 

When other evidence was offered, however, that the defendant was later found drinking the contents of these bottles, the conviction was upheld. (255 S.W. 433.)

DEPOSITIONS IN CRIMINAL CASES 

A deposition is the statement of a witness which has been put in writing according to requirements of law. In all criminal cases, the defendant has the right to be confronted by his ac cusers according to the Texas Constitution, Art. I, Sec. 10. 

Therefore, depositions may not be taken by the state and introduced as evidence against the defendant. Art. 734 C.C.P. states that a defendant may take depositions when an examination takes place in a criminal action, provided the defendant consents that the entire statement may also be used against him by the state on the trial of the case.

Art. 749 C.C.P. requires that the witness whose deposition has been taken must be unavailable to testify in court because one of the following conditions is present: 

1. The witness resides out of the state; or 

2. That since his deposition was taken, the witness has died; or 3. That he has removed beyond the limits of the state; or 

4. That he has been prevented from attending the court through the act or agency of the defendant; or 

5. By the act or agency of any person whose object was to deprive the defendant of the benefit of the testimony; or

6. That by reason of age or bodily infirmity such witness cannot attend.

Where the deposition is sought to be used by the state, the oath may be made by any credible person. When sought to be used by the defendant, the oath shall be made by him in person. 

Not knowing the whereabouts of a witness is not sufficient to introduce his deposition. (12 Tex.Cr.R. 352.) Depositions taken in a civil suit are inadmissible in a criminal trial. (28 

S.W. 533.)

TESTIMONY AT FORMER TRIAL OR HEARING 

Statements made in evidence on a previous judicial proceeding may be received in a subsequent trial as evidence of the truth of those statements provided the requirements of Art. 

750 C.C.P. are complied with: 

1. The accused had the privilege afforded him of crossexamining at the previous trial, examining court or inquest; 

2. The witness who testified previously is now dead, insane or beyond the jurisdiction of the court, or is prevented from attending.

Such evidence does not violate the constitutional right of the accused to be confronted by the witnesses against him. (35 

Tex. 587.) Only temporary absence of a witness from the state is not sufficient to introduce his testimony, but where it is indefinite as to when he will return, his testimony is admissible. (268 S.W. 2d 136.)

DYING DECLARATIONS (ART. 725 C.C.P.) 

It is possible to obtain the dying declaration of a person who is the victim of a homicide. The declaration is admissible only in a criminal case. The following are requisites of a dying declaration: 

1. It must be made by the victim of the homicide. 

2. At the time the victim makes the statement, he must be conscious of his approaching death and believe there is no hope of recovery. 

3. The statement must be made voluntarily and not through any persuasion. 

4. The statement must not be made in answer to questions calculated to lead the victim to make any particular statement. 

5. The victim must be sane at the time he makes the statement; and the statement may be oral or written.

Dying declarations are admissible when they relate to the identity of the persons participating in the homicide, the circumstances surrounding the homicide, or the transaction from which death resulted. When they pertain to unrelated matters, they are inadmissible. Dying declarations are usually offered into evidence by the state where the victim has identified his assailant.

ADMISSIONS 

An admission is any act or statement, by a party or his authorized agent, which is inconsistent with his present position in the courtroom. The reason that admissions are admissible into evidence is that a party ordinarily would not make statements or do things which would constitute admissions against himself. An admission is offered against the person making the statement, although at the time the statement was made it may have been to his favor and self-serving. An admission may be based on pure hearsay and the person declaring may now be dead or alive, however, it must be made by a party to the suit. 

Any act or conduct of a party which is inconsistent with his present position in court, is admissible in evidence against him, for example: that a defendant induced a witness to leave the state to prevent him from testifying, or attempted to intimidate a witness. (21 S.W. 2d 41.)

In civil cases, there is a type of admission called "admission by silence." If a statement were made which ordinarily called for a reply such as an answer to a question and the person had an opportunity to reply, and it is certain that the party heard and understood it, then the fact that the party remained silent is some evidence of an admission as to the truth of the statement.

In criminal cases, there can be no admission by silence. 

Silence is no admission against a person when that person is under arrest, because he is not obliged to say anything, and if he does, it must be put in writing before it is binding upon him. 

(Art. 727 C.C.P.) DECLARATION AGAINST INTEREST 

In civil cases only, a party to the suit may make a declaration against his pecuniary or proprietary interest, provided the party is speaking from firsthand knowledge and his statement is not based on hearsay. It must be shown there was no motive to misrepresent the facts. The statement which is offered as a declaration against interest need not be made by the party to the suit, however, the declarant must be dead at the time of the trial, or for all practical purposes, must be unavailable as

a witness so that his testimony is otherwise unobtainable. A declaration against interest would arise, for example, where a person struck by a train, admitted, shortly before he died, that he was asleep when the train struck him. (78 S.W. 556.)

DECLARATION AGAINST PENAL INTEREST 

The declaration against penal interest is a relatively uncommon exception to the hearsay rule. In a circumstantial evidence case where the accused is being tried for a homicide, for example, it is legitimate to introduce evidence that another party was in a position to have committed the homicide. Motive and other matters of that character are also admissible to show a reason why another and not the accused party committed the crime. (114 S.W. 814.) The declaration of a third party that he committed the offense is admissible in favor of the accused. 

Such statements are allowable, although they are purely hearsay, on the principle that one would not make a statement subjecting himself to criminal prosecution if it were false. The statements may be oral as well as written, and the person making the statement need not be dead at the time the statements are offered into evidence.

CONFESSIONS 

In the Texas courts, confessions are subject to rigid scrutiny and in order that they be admissible against an accused, the statutory requirements must be complied with exactly. Art. 

726 C.C.P. states that the confession of a defendant may be used in evidence against him if it appears that the confession was freely made and without compulsion or persuasion. The state has the burden of proving that the confession was given voluntarily.

A confession will be received into evidence if it follows the requirements set forth in Art. 727 C.C.P. which stipulates the following: 

1. The person making the confession must be warned that he does not have to make a confession, and that any statement he makes may be used against him. 

2. He must be warned by the person to whom he gives the confession. He may not be warned by one person and give the confession to another. 

3. It must be a voluntary confession, one not made while under illegal detention, by use of force, fear, threats or promise of reward. In order for a promise to invalidate a confession, it must be made by someone with apparent authority to fulfill the promise.

888 

4. The confession must be signed by the accused. 

5. The statute does not require that there be any witnesses to the confession unless the defendant is unable to write his name, and instead, signs with a mark. It is, however, a good practice to have witnesses to a confession who are not peace officers. In case the defendant denies that the confession was voluntary, it is possible to introduce the testimony of the witnesses who saw the defendant sign the confession in order to prove it was voluntary. 

Although a confession may be held inadmissible due to some technicality or failure to comply with some part of the statute, still, a confession may furnish information which leads to the discovery of facts relating to the crime, such as stolen property, the murder weapon, etc. These facts and evidence so discovered, are admissible into evidence regardless of the admissibility of the confession itself. But, where the confession was obtained through the use of brutality or violence, even the "fruits of the confession," as they are called, are inadmissible into evidence, due to the illegally obtained confession. (208 S.W. 2d 643.)

Where a confession is secured by threats, fear, or violence, it is inadmissible in court. Where a confession so secured, is followed by a second confession which was voluntarily obtained from the defendant, but while he was still laboring under the fear, or suffering under the influence of brutality or violence exerted to obtain the first confession, neither confession will be admissible into evidence. (160 S.W. 2d 260.) Fear of legal punishment is not sufficient to render a confession invalid; and mere advice that the defendant should tell the truth or confess and plead guilty will not invalidate a confession. 

REPUTATION 

Evidence of reputation is ordinarily based upon hearsay, but it is admissible into the trial of a case as an exception to the rule prohibiting hearsay. The type of reputation which is most often used in Texas courts is the reputation which a person has in the community or district in which he lives or is best known. 

Before evidence of reputation is admissible, the court must decide whether the matter is of such a general or public interest to the community that a prolonged and constant discussion would sift the possible errors and give rise to a fairly trustworthy report on a man's reputation. To be admissible, the testimony must purport to give the final opinion of the community as distinguished from a mere rumor or a report which is not credited.

Traits of character are provable by reputation, that is, moral character involving traits of honesty, truthfulness, etc. Sanity or insanity, however, cannot be established by general reputation. Examples of facts which can be proved by reputation are: the fact of military service, a general custom, the race of a particular person, marriage, birth, and death, and also the time and place of the foregoing. A person's age may also be established by reputation. Pedigree, or relationship, on the other hand, must be proved by the declarations of a deceased person who was related by blood or marriage to the person whose pedigree or relationship is in issue; the general reputation of the community is not sufficient here. 

PRIVILEGED COMMUNICATIONS 

Certain communications are said to be privileged and are confidential, and, therefore, are not the subject of inquiry even by the courts. The oldest of such privileges is the confidential relationship between attorney and client. The reason for this rule is obvious; if a defendant in a criminal trial were to admit to his attorney that he was guilty, it would be a simple matter to obtain evidence of guilt from the defendant's attorney. This would prevent the defendant from receiving a fair trial and obstruct the orderly handling of any case where a defendant had to be cautious in what he related to his counsel. 

In Texas, privileged communications are those made between attorney and client, and between husband and wife; but the privilege does not extend to the physician-patient or confessorpenitent relationships. An attorney-at-law shall not disclose a communication made to him by his client during the existence of that relationship, nor disclose any other fact which came to the knowledge of that attorney by reason of that relationship. 

The privilege is personal to the client and may be claimed by him alone. A party who is not the client may not raise the objection, nor may the claim be made by the attorney. This means that if the client does not wish the information divulged in court, he may object to such testimony; no one else may raise the objection. Conversely, if the client wishes the information divulged, it is permissible to waive the privilege and admit the communication. When the client relies on the privilege and does not wish the communication divulged, the opposing counsel may not comment upon the invoking of this privilege in his argument to the jury.

The privilege between husband and wife arises where the communication was induced by the marital relationship, and was not such a statement that would be made to third parties. If the

statement were voluntarily made in the presence of children old enough to comprehend, or to other members of the family within the intimacy of the family circle, the statements are not privileged. Once the privilege comes into existence, it is permanent and is not lost by the termination of the marital relationship, whether by death, divorce or separation, except in divorce proceedings between husband and wife themselves, or in a criminal case wherein one of the spouses has committed an offense against the other. Art. 714 C.C.P. states that neither husband nor wife shall, in any case, testify as to communications made by one to the other while married; nor shall they, after the marriage relation ceases, be made witnesses as to any such communication made while the marriage relation existed, except in a case where one or the other is prosecuted for an offense; and a declaration or communication made by the wife to the husband, or by the husband to the wife, goes to extenuate or to justify an offense for which either is on trial. The husband and wife may, in all criminal actions, be witnesses for each other, but they shall in no case testify against each other except in a criminal prosecution for an offense committed by one against the other. Ordinarily, the spouse may waive the privilege; however, the statute prohibits one spouse from testifying against the other in a criminal prosecution, whether he wishes to waive the privilege or not, except where the offense has been committed by one spouse against the other.

SELF-INCRIMINATION Under federal law, the Fifth Amendment to the U.S. Con stitution provides that a defendant shall not be compelled to give evidence against himself, and this privilege has been carried over into the Texas Constitution, in Art. I, Section 10. In all criminal prosecutions, the accused is entitled to a speedy public trial by an impartial jury. He shall have a right to demand the nature and the cause of the accusation, and he shall not be compelled to give evidence against himself, and shall have the right to be heard by himself or counsel, and shall have the right to be confronted by the witnesses against him. 

In a criminal case, that is, the actual trial itself, a defendant is not required to give evidence against himself; he is not required to testify in court, or be sworn, and no comment may be made during the case concerning his failure to take the witness stand.

Anything which does not make the defendant a witness against himself, does not violate the privilege against selfincrimination. The following are examples of evidence which

a defendant may be compelled to give without violating the privilege: appearing in a suspect line-up, inspection of the defendant's body; standing up in court room, putting on hat or glasses, etc., for purposes of identification (69 S.W. 165); the defendant can be compelled to write his name (48 S.W. 2d 632); and fingernail scrapings may be taken (209 S.W. 2d 925); he may be fingerprinted (115 S.W. 2d 681); and his shoes can be removed and placed in plaster casts for identification purposes (190 S.W. 157.)

Where a person was arrested for driving while intoxicated and was compelled by the arresting officers to walk, make sudden turns, place his finger on his nose, answer questions, and where a chemical analysis of urine was taken, all against the will of the defendant, such coercion was held to violate the privilege against self-incrimination. (146 S.W. 2d 381.) In gathering evidence from the defendant himself, it is difficult to determine exactly when his privilege against self-incrimination is being violated. Compulsion is the keynote of the rule. The courts hold that when the body of the defendant is the source of the evidence, it is admissible. When the evidence is taken against the will of the defendant there is a possible violation, whereas taking the evidence voluntarily or in the absence of actual, physical compulsion, the defendant's rights are probably not violated.

Where a paraffin test was taken on the accused by covering his hands with melted paraffin and then removed and examined, the results of this test, which showed that the defendant had nitrates on his hands, were admissible. The witness gave his opinion that the defendant had recently fired a gun. The evidence was admissible even though the paraffin test was taken without the consent of the defendant. (266 S.W. 2d 864.)

Evidence acquired by virtue of the taking of a urine test from a defendant is admissible if voluntarily given, (175 S.W. 

2d 258); and evidence obtained through the use of "intoximeter" tests is admissible if voluntarily given. (261 S.W. 2d 161).

Where a physician examined the defendant accused of rape, the examiner was permitted to testify to his discovery of a venereal disease. (256 S.W. 289.) The court cannot require the prosecutrix to be examined by physicians appointed by the court to ascertain whether she has led a virtuous life.

IMMUNITY 

An agreement not to prosecute, or the promise of immunity, in return for a promise to testify is not binding unless it is approved by the court. Neither is it binding where the defendant does testify under a promise of immunity and he testifies falsely.

(52 S.W. 83.) Where the promise of immunity is given merely by the district attorney or a sheriff not to prosecute, the agreement is not legally sufficient upon which to base a claim for immunity, yet, it has been held sufficient to bind the state not to use the testimony. (137 S.W. 2d 782.) The state, in justice and in fairness, is required to respect these agreements. 

ILLEGALLY OBTAINED EVIDENCE 

Evidence which is illegally obtained is inadmissible in court. 

For a discussion of illegally obtained evidence, see section on 

Search and Seizure.

CHAPTER Juvenile Delinquency DELINQUENCY STATISTICS 

Statistics concerning juvenile delinquency are alarming. And since the delinquent, if not properly directed during his youth, is apt to become a greater problem to the law enforcement officer as a criminal, the control of delinquency is a major factor in the crime prevention program of most law enforcement agencies. According to statistics of the Children's Bureau, U. S. 

Department of Health, Education and Welfare, it has been shown that the police force deals with more delinquent children than does any other community agency. In 1952, for example, an estimated one million young people were dealt with by the police because of alleged delinquency. Some 385,000 children were brought before the juvenile courts, and 40,000 were sent to training schools. Peace officers are primarily concerned with delinquency as distinguished from neglect and dependency cases of juveniles which are ordinarily handled by other community agencies.

THE ATTITUDE OF THE LAW 

The attitude of the law, and consequently that of the law enforcement officer toward juveniles, is one which recognizes that children are not adults and should not be held accountable for their behavior in the same way that adults are. In the case of delinquent children society has taken a position which is designed for the protection and rehabilitation rather than the punishment of the offenders. For this reason state laws have been enacted in accord with this view. The law regards delinquents not as criminals but as immature youths in need of corrective assistance by the state. Delinquency arises, usually, where the family has been unable to guide the children, properly so that they become a problem for the state to handle. The concept underlying the juvenile delinquency acts in most states is that the government must step in and exercise guardianship over a child found under such adverse social or individual conditions as develop crime.

When the law enforcement officer realizes that the attitude of the law toward juveniles is one designed for protection and rehabilitation, the approach of the officer toward children in the line of duty is made clear. The effect that an officer can have upon a child whether in a casual meeting or where the child is being cautioned, can be profound. The officer represents the

authority of the community, and his behavior toward children does a great deal to form the child's attitude toward law enforcement. A negative approach wherein the officer represents "punishment" provokes fear, hatred, and revolt. Kindness and understanding, on the other hand, even when accompanied by firmness, will instill in the child a respect for authority and trust in law enforcement officers.

Where the juvenile is defiant and aggressive and represents a threat to personal safety, as many delinquents do, the officer should not fail to use appropriate and reasonable means to protect himself and the community. This is the officer's duty. Still, understanding the behavior characteristics of children will aid immeasurably in curbing delinquency and crime. Recognizing the child's attitude is important particularly when he is being interviewed regarding some alleged violation of law. The officer must investigate not only the acts done but also the background of the child himself. The officer is not seeking information which will result in a conviction as with an adult accused of crime. Instead, the officer is seeking facts which will establish delinquency, if such is the case, and moreover, what should be done with the child in order to aid him in conforming to the demands of society.

JUVENILE DELINQUENCY ACT IN TEXAS Prior to the enactment of the Juvenile Delinquency Act in 

Texas, the trials of juvenile offenders were considered to be criminal cases. In 1943, the present Juvenile Delinquency Act was passed by the Texas Legislature. It is a civil statute, not a penal statute. (Art. 2338 R.C.S.) The purpose of this Act is to secure for each child under its jurisdiction, such care, guidance, and control, preferably in his own home, as will serve the child's welfare and the best interest of the state; and when such child is removed from his own family, to secure for him custody, care, and discipline as nearly as possible equivalent to that which should have been given him by his parents. (Art. 2338-1 R.C.S.)

DELINQUENT CHILD DEFINED 

A "delinquent child" is defined as any female person over the age of ten years and under the age of eighteen, and any male person over the age of ten years and under the age of seventeen: 

1. Who violates any penal law of this state of the grade of felony; or 

2. Who violates any penal law of this state of the grade of misdemeanor where the punishment prescribed for such offense may be by confinement in jail; or

JUVENILE DELINQUENCY 

45 3. Who habitually violates any penal law of this state of the grade of misdemeanor where the punishment prescribed for such offense is by pecuniary fine only; or 

4. Who habitually violates any penal ordinance of a political subdivision of the state; or 

5. Who habitually violates a compulsory school attendance law; or 

6. Who habitually so deports himself as to injure or endanger the morals or health of himself or of others; or 

7. Who habitually associates with vicious and immoral per sons. (Art. 2338-3 R.C.S.) THE JUVENILE COURT 

The juvenile court as defined by the Juvenile Delinquency Act, is a civil court rather than a criminal court. It is presided over by a district judge who acts as the juvenile judge. This court has exclusive original jurisdiction in proceedings governing any delinquent child. Once this court acquires jurisdiction, it retains it until the child has reached the age of twenty-one unless discharged prior to that time. (Art. 2338-5 R.C.S.)

The judge may conduct the hearing in the juvenile court in an informal manner, and the general public may be excluded so as to make the hearing private. A jury may be demanded in such hearings. The purpose of the hearing is to determine whether or not the child in question is, or is not, a "delinquent" according to the facts involved. If the child is found to be "delinquent" then the court may enter an order and proceed as follows: 

1. Place the child on probation or under supervision in his own home or in the custody of a relative or other fit person, upon such terms as the court shall determine; or 

2. Commit the child to a suitable public institution or agency, or to a suitable private institution authorized to care for children, or to place the child in a suitable family or parental home.

EFFECT OF JUVENILE DELINQUENCY ACT 

As a further indication that the intent of the Juvenile Delinquency Act is for protection and rehabilitation rather than punishment, the Act provides that no adjudication in the juvenile court imposes upon the child any civil disabilities as are ordinarily incurred by a criminal conviction, nor is any child deemed a criminal by reason of such adjudication. The Act specifically exempts all juveniles within the age bracket provided from any charge or conviction for any crime. Adjudication in the juvenile court does not constitute a conviction. Evidence

given in the juvenile court cannot be offered as evidence against the child in any case or proceeding in any other court except in another juvenile court. (Art. 2338-13 R.C.S.) Juveniles are not subject to being incarcerated in any jail or lockup in which persons other than juveniles are detained. (Art. 2338-17 R.C.S.)

INTERPRETATION OF THE JUVENILE DELINQUENCY ACT The Texas Juvenile Delinquency Act was held constitutional by the Supreme Court in 1944 in the case of Dendy vs. Wilson (142 Tex. 460.) This and other cases have added a number of significant features to the interpretation of the Act. As in any criminal case, a person is not required to give evidence against himself. A juvenile in the juvenile court is entitled to the privilege against self-incrimination unless he is granted immunity. 

The age of the juvenile when he commits an offense and when he is brought before either the juvenile court or a criminal court, is worthy of note. It is immaterial at what age a person commits an offense; if he is apprehended and brought to trial while he is within the age bracket provided for in the Juvenile Delinquency Act, the case must be referred to the juvenile court. 

If at the time of trial, however, the accused is over the statutory age limits (seventeen for boys, eighteen for girls) the criminal court would have jurisdiction. It could hardly be contended that one who had committed a heinous crime such as murder, while fifteen or sixteen years of age, and who was not apprehended or indicted until past twenty-one, would go absolutely unwhipped of justice by reason of this lapse of time. (85 Tex.Cr. R. 31.)

Hence, one who commits a criminal offense while a juvenile can be tried and punished for it provided the trial takes place after the juvenile has reached the maximum age limits of the Juvenile Delinquency Act; and provided further, that the person has not been placed in jeopardy for the offense by virtue of a hearing in the juvenile court. 

An example of the above provisions is the following: A girl committed murder at the age of seventeen. At the same time, she was in the act of committing robbery. The girl was apprehended. The right to a speedy trial made it impossible to hold the girl on a murder charge until she became eighteen and subject to criminal prosecution; nonetheless, she was held for the robbery and declared a delinquent under the Juvenile Delinquency Act. When she reached the age of eighteen she was indicted, tried, and convicted in the criminal court for the murder. (235 S.W. 2d 138.)

JUVENILE DELINQUENCY 

47 It should be noted that even though the Juvenile Delinquency Act exempts juveniles from criminal prosecution, they are not prohibited from being qualified as witnesses in court solely because of age. Children are subject to the pains and penalties of perjury. (147 Tex. Cr.R. 554.)

In a juvenile court hearing, the facts essential to an adjudication of delinquency need not be established beyond a reasonable doubt as in a conviction for an offense in a criminal court, but must be established by some evidence having probative force. (269 S.W. 2d 956.)

Art. 31 P.C. is not affected by the Juvenile Delinquency Act. 

The code provides that no person shall be punished by death if the offense were committed before he reached the age of seventeen. Thus, a boy of seventeen can be convicted of a crime and punished the same as a criminal except that he may not receive the death penalty.

The act of contributing to the delinquency of a child is made an offense under Art. 534a P.C., and the juvenile court has jurisdiction to try this matter.

Art. 802d P.C. 

A special statute, Art. 802d P.C., has been enacted relating to juveniles who are found to be driving while intoxicated, or violating speed laws. This article applies notwithstanding the 

Juvenile Delinquency Act. 

Sec. 1. Any minor who has reached his or her fourteenth birthday but has not reached his or her seventeenth birthday and who drives or operates an automobile or any other motor vehicle upon any public road or highway in this state, or upon any street or alley within the limits of an incorporated city, town or village, in a reckless manner, at an excessive rate of speed, or while under the influence of intoxicating liquors, as hereinafter defined in this Act, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and upon conviction shall be punished by a fine of not less than $1.00 nor more than $50.00. 

Sec. 2. (a) Any minor who drives any vehicle in willful or wanton disregard of the rights or safety of others or without due caution or circumspection, and at a speed or in a manner so as to endanger or be likely to endanger a person or property, shall be guilty of reckless driving. (The language of this section is vague and indefinite and is probably unconstitutional. 

251 S.W. 2d 890.)

(b) Any minor who operates a motor vehicle at a speed in excess of the maximum speed allowable under existing law, shall be guilty of speeding. (c) Any minor who drives or operates an automobile or any other vehicle while such person is intoxicated or under the influence of intoxicating liquors, shall be guilty of driving or operating a motor vehicle while under the influence of intoxicating liquors. 

Sec. 3. Provided that for good cause shown, and when it shall appear to the satisfaction of the court that the ends of justice and the best interest of the public as well as the defendant will be subserved thereby, the courts of the State of Texas having original jurisdiction of such criminal actions shall have the power after conviction or plea of guilty to suspend the imposition of such fine and may place the defendant on probation for a period of ninety days. Any such minor placed on probation shall be under the supervision of such court. 

Sec. 4. Nothing contained in this Act shall be construed to repeal or affect any other statutes regulating the powers and duties of juvenile courts; the provisions of this Act shall be cumulative with all other Acts on this subject.

CHAPTER Fees and Wages of Peace Officers SECTION 1-FEES IN CRIMINAL PROCEEDINGS RECORD OF FEES COLLECTED 

Each sheriff, constable, and marshal must keep a fee book and enter therein all fees charged for service rendered in any criminal action or proceeding. The book may be inspected by any person interested in such costs. (Art. 1009 C.C.P.) The fee book must show the number and style of the action or proceeding in which the costs are charged, and must name the officer or person to whom such costs are due, and state each item of costs separately. (Art. 1010 C.C.P.) No item of costs shall be taxed for a purported service which was not performed, or for a service for which no fee is expressly provided by law. (Art. 1011 

C.C.P.) No costs shall be payable by any person until there be produced, or ready to be produced, unto the person chargeable with the same, a written bill containing the items of such costs, signed by the officer to whom such costs are due or by whom the same are charged. (Art. 1013 C.C.P.)

NUMBER OF FEES CHARGEABLE Art. 1019a C.C.P. Fees in Felony Cases Against Same Defendant. 

In all felony cases where any officer is allowed fees payable by the state for services performed either before or after indictment, including examining trials before magistrates and habeas corpus proceedings, no officer shall be entitled to fees in more than five cases against the same defendant; provided, however, that where defendants are indicted and tried separately after severance of their cases, said officers shall be entitled to fees in five cases against each of said defendants, the same as if indicted and tried separately for separate offenses; provided further, that cases in which the same defendant has previously been indicted, tried, and convicted prior to the date of any act or acts for which said defendant is again apprehended, indicted, and/or tried, shall not be computed in determining the number of cases against such defendant in which such officers are entitled to collect fees.

FEES IN EXAMINING COURT Art. 1020 C.C.P. Fees in Examining Court. 

Sheriffs and constables serving process and attending any examining court in the examination of any felony case, shall

be entitled to such fees as are fixed by law for similar services in misdemeanor cases in county court to be paid by the state, not to exceed $4.00 in any one case, and mileage actually and necessarily traveled in going to the place of arrest, and for conveying the prisoner or prisoners to jail as provided in Art. 

1029 and Art. 1030 C.C.P., as the facts may be, but no mileage whatever shall be paid for summoning or attaching witnesses in the county where case is pending. Provided no sheriff or constable shall receive from the state any additional mileage for any subsequent arrest of a defendant in the same case, or in any other case in an examining court or in any district court based upon the same charge or upon the same criminal act, or growing out of the same criminal transaction, whether the arrest is made with or without a warrant, or before or after indictment, and in no event shall he be allowed to duplicate his fees for mileage for making arrests, with or without warrant, or when two or more warrants of arrest or capiases are served or could have been served on the same defendant on any one day.

Only one fee shall be allowed to any officer mentioned herein for services rendered in an examining trial, though more than one defendant is joined in the complaint, or a severance is had. 

No more than one fee shall be allowed to any officer where more than one case is filed against the same defendant for offenses growing out of the same criminal act or transaction. The account of the officer and the approval of the district judge must affirmatively show that the provisions of this article have been complied with.

FEES TO SHERIFF OR CONSTABLE 

Art. 1029 C.C.P. Fees to Sheriff or Constable.

In each county where there have been cast at the preceding presidential election 3,000 votes or more, the sheriff and constable shall receive the following fees: 

1. For executing each warrant of arrest, capias, for making arrest without warrant when so authorized by law, the sum of $1.00. In all cases, for each mile actually and necessarily traveled in going to the place of arrest; and for conveying each prisoner to jail, he shall receive the mileage provided in subdivision 4. 

2. For summoning or attaching each witness, 50 cents. 

3. For summoning a jury in each case where a jury is actually sworn in, $2.00. 

4. For removing or conveying prisoners, for each mile going and coming, including guards and all other neces-

sary expenses, when traveling by railroad, 10 cents. When traveling otherwise than by railroad, 14 cents. Provided that where more than one prisoner is so conveyed or removed at the same time, in addition to the foregoing, he shall be allowed for each additional prisoner, per mile, 8 cents. 

5. For each mile the officer may be compelled to travel in executing criminal process, summoning or attaching witnesses, 5 cents. Provided that in no case shall he be allowed to duplicate his mileage when two or more witnesses are named in the same or different writs in any case or series of cases against the same defendant, or in companion cases, or otherwise; when it is possible to serve process on them in the same neighborhood or vicinity during the same trip, he shall not charge mileage for serving such witnesses to or from the county seat, but shall charge only one mileage and for such additional miles only as are actually and necessarily traveled in summoning each additional witness. 

6. For service of criminal process, not otherwise provided for, in going and returning he shall receive per mile, 5 cents. If two or more persons are mentioned in the same or different writs, the rule prescribed in subdivision 5 shall apply. 

7. For conveying witnesses attached by him to any court, or in habeas corpus proceedings out of his county, or when directed by the judge from any other county to the court where the case is pending, $1.50 per day for each day actually and necessarily consumed in going to and returning from such courts, and his actual and necessary expenses by the nearest practical route, or nearest practical public conveyance, the amount to be stated by him. in an account which shall show the place where the witnesses were attached, the distance to the nearest railroad station, and miles actually traveled to each court; if horses or vehicles are used, from whom hired and price paid and length of time consumed and paid out for feeding horses, and to whom; if meals and lodgings are provided from whom and when, and price paid; provided that officers shall not be entitled to receive exceeding 15 cents per meal, and 35 cents per night for lodging for any witness. No item for expenses shall be allowed, unless the officer present with his account to the officer whose duty it is to approve the same, a written receipt for each item of account, except as to such items as are furnished by the officer himself. When meals and lodgings are furnished

by the officer in person, conveying the witness, he shall be allowed to receive not exceeding 25 cents per meal, and 

25 cents per night for lodging. Each said receipt shall be filed with the clerk of the court approving such accounts. 

Said accounts shall also show, before said officer shall be entitled to compensation for expenses of attached witnesses, that, before starting with said witnesses to the foreign court, he carried each of them before the magistrate nearest place of serving the attachment, giving his name and residence, and that said witness made oath in writing before said magistrate, certified copies of which shall be attached to the account, that they were unable to give bond for their appearance at court, or refused to give bond after having been advised by said officer of their right to do so. The officer shall also present to the court the affidavit of the witness to the same effect, or shall show that the witness refused to make the affidavit; and should it appear to the court that the witness is willing and able to give bond, the sheriff shall not be entitled to any compensation for conveying such witness. All accounts for fees in criminal cases, by sheriffs, shall be sworn to by the said officer, and shall state that said account is true, just and correct in every particular, and be presented to the judge, who shall during such term of court, carefully examine such account and, if found to be correct, in whole or in part, shall so certify and allow the same for such amount as he may find to be correct. If allowed by him in whole or in part, he shall so certify; and such account, with the affidavit of the sheriff, and certificate of the judge, shall be recorded by the district clerk in a book kept by him for that purpose, which shall constitute a part of the proceedings or minutes of the court. 

The clerk shall certify to the original account, and shall show that the same has been recorded, and said account shall then become due, and the same shall constitute a voucher on which the comptroller is authorized to issue a warrant, if such account, when presented to the comptroller, shall be accompanied by a certified copy under the hand and seal of the district clerk, of the returns made on the process for which such officer is claiming fees, corresponding to the amount so claimed in his account. The minutes of the court above provided for, or a certified copy thereof, may be used in evidence against the officer making the affidavit, for perjury, in case said affidavit shall be willfully false. When the officer receiving the writ for the attachment of such a witness shall take bond for the ap-

33 pearance of such witness he shall be entitled to receive from the state $1.00 for each bond so taken; but he shall be responsible to the court issuing said writ, that the said bond is in proper form, and has been executed by the witness with one or more good and solvent sureties; and said bond shall in no case be less than $100.00. The comptroller may require from such officer a certified copy of all such process before auditing any account. When no inquest or examining trial has been held at which sufficient evidence is taken upon which to find an indictment, which fact shall be certified by the grand jury, or when the grand jury shall state to the district judge that an indictment cannot be procured except upon testimony of nonresident witnesses, the district judge may have attachments issued to other counties for witnesses not to exceed the number for which the sheriff may receive pay as provided for by law, to testify before grand juries; provided, that the judge shall not approve the accounts of any sheriff for more than one witness to any one fact, nor more than three witnesses to any one case pending before the grand jury, in which case the sheriff shall receive the same compensation as he does for conveying attached witnesses before the court. Subdivision 7 of this article shall apply to the officer affected thereby in all counties in Texas. 

8. For attending a prisoner on habeas corpus, for each day, $4.00 together with mileage as provided in subdivision 4 when removing such prisoner out of the county, under an order issued by a district or appellate judge. 

Art. 1030 C.C.P. Fees to Sheriff or Constable.

In each county where there have been cast at the preceding presidential election less than 3,000 votes, the sheriff or constable shall receive the following fees when the charge is a felony: 

1. For executing each warrant of arrest or capias, or for making arrest without warrant, when authorized by law, the sum of $1.00, and 5 cents for each mile actually and necessarily traveled in going to place of arrest, and for conveying the prisoner or prisoners to jail, mileage, as provided for in subdivision 4 shall be allowed, provided, that in counties that have a population of less than 40,000 inhabitants, as shown by the preceding federal census, the following fees shall apply: For executing each warrant of arrest or capias, or for making arrest without warrant, when authorized by law, $3.00, and 15 cents

for each mile actually and necessarily traveled in going to place of arrest, and for conveying prisoners to jail, mileage as provided for in subdivision 4 shall be allowed; and $1.00 shall be allowed for the approval of a bond. 

2. For summoning or attaching each witness, 50 cents; provided that in counties that have a population of less than 40,000 inhabitants, as shown by the preceding federal census, the following fees shall apply: For summoning or attaching each witness, 50 cents, and where a bond is required of said witness, for approval of said bond, $1.00. 

3. For summoning jury in each case, where jury is actually sworn in, $2.00. 

4. For removing a prisoner, for each mile going and coming, including guards and all other expenses, when traveling by railroad, 10 cents; when traveling otherwise than by railroad, 15 cents; provided, that when more than one prisoner is removed at the same time, in addition to the foregoing, he shall only be allowed 10 cents a mile for each additional prisoner. 

5. For each mile the officer may be compelled to travel in executing criminal process, summoning or attaching witnesses, 5 cents; provided that in no case shall he be allowed to duplicate his mileage when two or more witnesses are named in the same or different writs in any case, and he shall serve process on them in the same vicinity or neighborhood during the same trip; he shall not charge mileage for serving such witness to and from the county seat, but shall only charge one mileage and for such additional miles only as are actually and necessarily traveled in summoning or attaching each additional witness. When process is sent by mail to any officer away from the county seat or returned by mail by such officer, he shall only be allowed to charge mileage for the miles actually traveled by him in executing such process, and the return of the officer shall show the character of the service and miles actually traveled in accordance with this subdivision; and his accounts shall show the facts; provided, that in counties that have a population of less than 40,000 inhabitants, as shown by the preceding federal census, the following fees shall apply: For each mile the officer may be compelled to travel in executing criminal process, summoning or attaching witnesses, 10 cents; provided, that in no case shall he be allowed to duplicate his mileage when two or more witnesses are named in the same or different writs in any case and he shall serve process on them in the

same vicinity or neighborhood during the same trip, he shall not charge mileage for serving such witness to and from the county seat, but shall only charge one mileage, and for such additional miles only as are actually and necessarily traveled in summoning or attaching each additional witness. When process is sent by mail to any officer away from the county seat or returned by mail by such officer, he shall only be allowed to charge mileage for the miles actually traveled by him in executing such process, and the return of the officer shall show the character of the service and the miles actually traveled in accordance with this subdivision; and his accounts shall show the facts. 

6. To officers for service of criminal process not otherwise provided for, the sum of 5 cents a mile going and returning, shall be allowed; provided, if two or more persons are mentioned in the same or different writs, the rules prescribed in subdivision 5 shall apply; provided, that in counties that have a population of less than 40,000 inhabitants, as shown by the preceding federal census, the following fees shall apply: To officers for service of criminal process not otherwise provided for, the sum of 10 cents a mile going and returning shall be allowed; provided, if two or more persons are mentioned in the same or different writs, the rule prescribed in subdivision 5 shall apply. 

7. For conveying a witness attached by him to any court, or grand jury, or in habeas corpus proceeding out of his county, or when directed by the judge from any other county, to the court where the case is pending, $1.00 per day for each day actually and necessarily consumed in going and returning from such court, and his actual necessary expenses, by the nearest practicable route or nearest practicable public conveyance, the amount to be stated by him in an account, which shall show the place at which the witness was attached, the distance to the nearest railroad station, and miles actually traveled to reach the court; if horses or vehicles are used, from whom hired, and price paid, and length of time consumed, and the amount paid out for feeding horses, and to whom; if meals and lodging were provided, from whom and when and price paid; provided that officers shall not be entitled to receive exceeding 50 cents per meal, and 35 cents per night for lodging for any witness. Said account shall also show, before said officer shall be entitled to compensation for expenses of attached witnesses, that, before starting

with said witnesses to the foreign court, he carried each of them before the magistrate nearest the place of serving the attachment, giving his name and residence, and that said witness made oath in writing before such magistrate, certified copies of which shall be attached to the account, that they were unable to give bond for their appearance at court, or refused to give bond after having been advised by said officer of their right to do so. The officer shall also present to the court the affidavit of the witness to the same effect or shall show that the witness refused to make the affidavit and, should it appear to the court that the witness was able and willing to give bond, the sheriff shall not be entitled to any compensation for conveying such witness; and said account shall be sworn to by the officer, and shall state that said account is true, just and correct in every particular, and present the same to the judge, who shall, during such term of court, carefully examine such account, and, if found to be correct, in whole or in part, shall so certify, and allow the same for such an amount as he may find to be correct; and, if by him allowed, in whole or in part, he shall so certify; and such account with the affidavit of the sheriff, and certificate of the judge, shall be recorded by the district clerk in a book to be kept by him for that purpose, which shall constitute a part of the proceedings or minutes of the court; and the clerk shall certify to the original account and shall show that the same has been so recorded; and said account shall then become due, and the same shall constitute a voucher, on which the comptroller is authorized to issue a warrant; and such minutes of the court, or a certified copy thereof, may be used in evidence against the officer making the affidavit, for perjury, in case said affidavit shall be willfully false. When the officer receiving a writ for the attachment of such witness shall take a bond for the appearance of any such witness he shall be entitled to receive from the state, $1.00 for each bond so taken; but he shall be responsible to the court issuing said writ, and said bond is in proper form, and has been executed by the witness with one or more good or solvent securities; and said bond shall, in no case, be less than $100.00. The comptroller may require from such officer a certified copy of all such process before auditing any such account. 

8. For attending a prisoner on habeas corpus, for each day, $4.00, together with mileage as provided in subdivision 5,

when removing such prisoner out of the county under an order issued by a district or appellate judge. 

FUGITIVES FROM JUSTICE 

Art. 1030a C.C.P. Allowance to Sheriffs and Deputies.

Sec. 1. Every sheriff, or deputy sheriff, in any county of this state, who shall hereafter arrest, or cause to be arrested, any person, or persons indicated for a criminal offense of the grade of felony, in the county where such officer is the duly acting sheriff, or deputy sheriff, shall be paid the sum of 5 cents per mile from the state line and return thereto, along the nearest practicable route, to the point where such person or persons have been, or will be, placed under arrest, and in addition thereto, such officer, or officers, shall be paid, not to exceed $5.00 per day, per person, for hotel bills, meals and other expenses necessarily contracted in the performance of such official duty. 

Sec. 2. The Comptroller of Public Accounts of the State of 

Texas is authorized and directed to pay, out of any fund or funds, provided for such purpose, upon the presentment of a duly itemized and verified mileage, per diem and expense account of any such officer, approved by the district judge of the district where such official duty was performed as provided in the preceding section, all of such account due, provided that only one claim for mileage shall be paid for any such trip, and further providing that not more than two such officers shall draw per diem and expense accounts for one of such trips. 

Sec. 5. The compensation herein provided for the sheriff or any deputy sheriff of the county shall be allowable to such officer as expenses of office, and shall not be included in his compensation, and/or salary paid him, as now authorized by law. 

Art. 1031 C.C.P. Services by Officer Other than Sheriff. 

When services have been rendered by any peace officer other than a sheriff, such as are enumerated in the two preceding articles, such officer shall receive the same fees therefor as are allowed the sheriff. The same shall be taxed in the sheriff's bill of costs, and noted therein as costs due such peace officer; and when received by such sheriff, he shall pay the same to such peace officer.

Art. 1032 C.C.P. Sheriff Shall Not Charge Fees. 

A sheriff shall not charge fees for arrests made by rangers, or mileage for prisoners transported by rangers, or mileage or other fees for transporting a witness under attachment issued from another county, unless such witness refuses to give bail for his appearance, or files an affidavit with such sheriff of his inability to give bail.

BILL OF COSTS Art. 1033 C.C.P. Officer Shall Make Out Cost Bill. 

Before the close of each term of the district court, the district or county attorney, sheriff and clerk of said court shall each make out a bill of the costs claimed to be due them by the state, respectively, in the felony cases tried at that term; the bill shall show: 

1. The style and number of each case. 

2. The offense charged against the defendant. 

3. The term of the court at which the case was disposed of. 

4. The disposition of the case, and that the case was finally disposed of, and no appeal taken. 

5. The name and number of defendants; and, if more than one, whether they were tried jointly or separately. 

6. Where each defendant was arrested, or witness served, stating the county in which the service was made, giving the distance and direction from county seat of county in which the process is served. 

7. The court shall inquire whether there have been several prosecutions for a transaction that is but one offense in law. If there is more than one prosecution for the same transaction, or a portion thereof, that could have been combined in one indictment against the same defendant, the judge shall allow fees to sheriffs, clerks and district and county attorneys in but one prosecution. 

8. Where the defendants in a case have severed on the trial, the judge shall not allow the charges for service of process and mileage to be duplicated in each case as tried; but only such additional fees shall be allowed as are caused by the severance.

CARE OF JURORS 

Each county shall be liable for the expense of food and lodging for jurors impaneled in a felony case. (Art. 1038 C.C.P.) 

A juror may pay his own expenses and draw his script; but the county is responsible in the first place for all expense incurred by the sheriff in providing suitable food and lodging for the jury, not to exceed $2.00 a day.

Art. 1042 C.C.P. Sheriff Reimbursed. 

The sheriff shall pay the expenses of jurors impaneled in cases of felony (except where they are paid by the juror himself), the expenses of employing and maintaining a guard, and to support and take care of all prisoners, for all of which, he shall be reimbursed by the proper county according to the rates fixed in the two preceding articles.

Art. 1043 C.C.P. Sheriff Shall Present Account. 

At each term of the district court of his county, the sheriff may present to the district judge presiding his accounts for all expenses incurred by him for food and lodging of jurors in case of trials for felony during the term at which his account is presented. Such account shall state the number and style of the cases in which the jurors were impaneled, and specify by name each juror's expenses paid by such sheriff, and the number of days the same were paid, and shall be verified by the affidavit of such sheriff.

Art. 1045 C.C.P. Judge Shall Give Sheriff Draft. 

The district judge shall give the sheriff a draft upon the county treasurer of the proper county for the amount of each account allowed by him; and the same, when presented to such treasurer, shall be paid in like manner as jury certificates are paid.

CUSTODY OF PRISONERS 

Art. 1040 C.C.P. Allowance to Sheriff for Prisoners.

For the safekeeping, support and maintenance of prisoners confined in jail or under guard, the sheriff shall be allowed the following charges: 

1. For the safekeeping of each prisoner for each day, the sum of 15 cents, not to exceed the sum of $200.00 per month. 

2. For support and maintenance, for each prisoner for each day, such an amount as may be fixed by the commissioners court, provided the same shall be reasonably sufficient for such purpose, and in no event shall it be less than 40 cents per day nor more than 75 cents per day for each prisoner.

The net profits shall constitute fees of office and shall be accounted for by the sheriff in his annual report as other fees now provided by law. The sheriff shall in such report furnish an itemized verified account of all expenditures made by him for feeding and maintenance of prisoners, accompanying such report with receipts and vouchers in support of such items of expenditure, and the difference between such expenditures and the amount allowed by the commissioners court shall be deemed to constitute the net profits for which said officer shall account as fees of office. 

3. For necessary medical bill and reasonable extra compensation for attention to a prisoner during sickness, such an amount as the commissioners court of the county where the prisoner is confined may determine to be just and proper.

4. For reasonable funeral expenses in case of death.

Art. 1041 C.C.P. Guards and Matrons. 

The sheriff shall be allowed for each guard or matron necessarily employed in the safekeeping of prisoners $2.50 for each day. No allowance shall be made for the board of such guard or matron, nor shall any allowance be made for jailer or turnkey, except in counties having a population in excess of 40,000 inhabitants according to the last federal census. In such counties of 40,000 or more inhabitants, the commissioners court may allow each jail guard, matron, jailer and turnkey $4.50 per day; provided that in counties having a population in excess of 70,000 inhabitants and less than 220,000 inhabitants, according to the last preceding federal census, the commissioners court of such counties may allow each jail guard, jailer, matron and turnkey a salary of not to exceed $187.50 per month; provided further that, in counties having a population in excess of 220,000 inhabitants, according to the last preceding federal census, each jail guard, matron, jailer, jail bookkeeper and turnkey shall be paid not less than $175.00 per month. 

Art. 1041a C.C.P. Chief Jailer or Turnkey. 

In all counties in this state having a population of 145,000 inhabitants and not more than 300,000 inhabitants according to the last or any future federal census, the commissioners court shall allow the chief jailer and/or turnkey who has the care and custody of persons in the county jail, not to exceed $8.00 per day, and shall allow each assistant jailer and/or turnkey who has the care and custody of prisoners in the county jail, not to exceed $6.50 per day, and not to exceed four assistant jailers and/or turnkeys and a matron for each jail. 

Art. 1046 C.C.P. Account for Keeping Prisoners. 

At each regular term of the commissioners court, the sheriff shall present to such court his account verified by his affidavit for the expense incurred by him since the last account presented for the safekeeping and maintenance of prisoners, including guards employed, if any. Such account shall state the name of each prisoner, each item of expense incurred on account of such prisoner, the date of each item, the name of each guard employed, the length of time employed and the purpose of such employment. 

Art. 1048 C.C.P. Expenses of Prisoner from Another County. 

If the expenses incurred are for the safekeeping and maintenance of a prisoner from another county, the sheriff shall make out a separate account therefor, and submit the same to the county judge of his county, who shall carefully examine the

same, write thereon his approval for such amount as he finds. correct and sign and date such approval officially. 

FEES TAXED AGAINST DEFENDANT Art. 1065 C.C.P. Peace Officers Entitled to Fees. 

The following fees shall be allowed the sheriff, or other peace officer performing the same services in misdemeanor cases, to be taxed against the defendant on conviction: 

1. For executing each warrant of arrest, or capias, or making arrest without warrant. 

2. For summoning each witness. $2.00 .75 

3. For serving any writ not otherwise provided for. 

4. For taking and approving each bond, and returning the same to the court house, when necessary. 1.00 1.50 

5. For each commitment or release. 1.00 

6.

Jury fee, in each case where a jury is actually summoned 1.00 

7. For attending a prisoner on habeas corpus, when such prisoner, upon a hearing, has been remanded to custody, or held to bail, for each day's attendance 4.00 

8. For conveying a witness attached by him to any court out of his county $4.00 for each day or fractional part thereof, and his actual necessary expenses by the nearest practicable public conveyance, the amount to be stated by said officer, under oath and approved by the judge of the court from which the attachment issued. 

9. For conveying a prisoner after conviction to the county jail, for each mile, going and coming, by the nearest practicable route by private conveyance, 10 cents a mile, or by railway, 72 cents a mile. 

10. For conveying a prisoner arrested on a warrant or capias issued from another county to the court or jail of the county from which the process was issued, for each mile traveled going and coming, by the nearest practicable route, 1212 cents. 

11. For each mile he may be compelled to travel in executing criminal process and summoning or attaching witness, 71/2 cents. For traveling in the service of process not otherwise provided for, the sum of 712 cents for each mile going and returning.

If two or more persons are mentioned in the same writ, or two or more writs in the same case, he shall charge only for the distance actually and necessarily traveled in the same.

OTHER FEES Art. 1067 C.C.P. Fees of Peace Officers. 

Constables, marshals or other peace officers who execute process and perform services for justices in criminal actions, shall receive the same fees allowed to sheriffs for the same services. 

Art. 1072 C.C.P. Officers in Examining Court. 

Sheriffs and constables serving process and attending any examining court in the examination of a misdemeanor case shall be entitled to such fees as are allowed by law for similar services in the trial of such cases, not to exceed $3.00 in any one case, to be paid by the defendant in case of final conviction. 

Art. 951 C.C.P. Commissions to Other Officers. 

The sheriff or other officer, except a justice of the peace or his clerk, who collects money for the state or county, except jury fees, under any provision of this Code, shall be entitled to retain five per cent thereof when collected.

For delivering a prisoner to the state penitentiary at Huntsville under a warrant of execution, the sheriff shall be entitled to the same compensation as is now allowed by law to sheriffs for removing or conveying prisoners under the provisions of 

Sec. 4 of Art. 1029 or 1030 C.C.P. (Art. 800 C.C.P.) For the return of a prisoner who has escaped after being sentenced, or who has escaped from the penitentiary, and who is under a sentence of execution, the sheriff or other officer or other person performing any service shall receive the same compensation as is provided for similar services under the provisions of 

Art. 1029 or 1030 C.C.P. (Art. 805-806 C.C.P.) SECTION 2-FEES IN CIVIL PROCEEDINGS COMMISSION, FEE BOOK AND EXTORTION 

No official who fails or refuses to take out a commission shall be entitled to collect or receive either from the state or from individuals any money as fees of office or compensation for official services. (Art. 3882 R.C.S.) 

Every officer entitled by law to charge fees for services shall keep a fee book, and shall enter therein all fees charged for services rendered, which fee book shall, at all times, be subject to the inspection of any person wishing to see the amount of fees therein charged. (Art. 3907 R.C.S.)

None of the fees mentioned in this title shall be payable to any person whomsoever until there be produced, or ready to be produced, unto the person so owing or chargeable with the same, a bill or account in writing containing the particulars of such

fees, signed by the clerk or officer to whom such fees are due, or by whom the same are charged, or by the successor in office, or legal representative of such clerk or officer. (Art. 3908 R.C.S.)

If any officer named in this title shall demand and receive any higher fees than are prescribed to them in this title, or any fees that are not allowed by this title, such officer shall be liable to the party aggrieved for fourfold the fees so unlawfully demanded and received by him. (Art. 3909 R.C.S.)

County judges, clerks of the district and county courts, sheriffs, justices of the peace, constables and notaries public of the several counties shall keep posted up, at all times, in a conspicuous place in their respective offices, a complete list of fees allowed by law to be charged by them respectively. (Art. 

3910 R.C.S.)

The annual maximum fees and salaries of sheriffs, constables and other officers, are set out in Art. 3883 R.C.S. et seq. according to population and tax valuation of the various counties. 

FEES CHARGEABLE Rule 125 T.C.P. Parties Responsible. 

Each party to a suit shall be liable to the officers of the court for all costs incurred by himself. 

Rule 126 T.C.P. Fee for Execution of Process, Demand.

No sheriff or constable shall be compelled to execute any process in civil cases coming from any county other than the one in which he is an officer, unless the fees allowed him by law for the service of such process shall be paid in advance; except when affidavit is filed, as provided by law or the Rules of Civil Procedure. The clerk issuing the process shall indorse thereon the words, "pauper oath filed," and sign his name officially below them; and the officer in whose hands such process is placed for service shall serve the same. 

Rule 128 T.C.P. Officers May Demand Payment.

Officers may demand payment of all costs due in each and every case pending in their respective courts, up to the adjournment of each term of said courts.

Rule 129 T.C.P. How Costs Collected. 

If any party responsible for costs fails or refuses to pay the same within ten days after demand for payment, the clerk or justice of the peace may make certified copy of the bill of costs then due, and place the same in the hands of the sheriff or constable for collection. All taxes imposed on law proceedings shall be included in the bill of costs. Such certified bill of costs shall have the force and effect of an execution. The removal of a case

by appeal shall not prevent the issuance of an execution for costs at the end of the term. 

Rule 130 T.C.P. Officer to Levy.

The sheriff or constable upon demand and failure to pay said bill of costs, may levy upon a sufficient amount of property of the person from whom said costs are due to satisfy the same, and sell such property as under execution.
