Where a credible person informs a peace officer that a felony has been committed and that the offender is about to escape, Art. 215 C.C.P. authorizes a peace officer to arrest without warrant. In the case under consideration, there was no testimony showing that the defendant was seen at Three Rivers at or about the time of the burglary. There was no testimony showing that the officers had been informed by a credible person that the defendant had committed or was connected with the commission of the burglary. 

There was no testimony that the defendant had committed a felony or a breach of the peace in the presence of either officers or a magistrate who verbally ordered the arrest. Moreover, there was no testimony from any source that it was shown by satisfactory proof to any peace officer upon the representation of a

credible person that a felony had been committed and the party was about to escape so that there was not time to procure a warrant. All that could be gathered from the testimony was that the officers had a suspicion that the defendant might have committed the offense. The suspicion proved to be correct thanks to an alert police force, but mere suspicion does not authorize an officer to arrest without the procurance of a warrant, nor does it authorize him to search the premises and possessions of the suspect. The fact that the party arrested without a warrant may justly have been suspected of a felony, is not sufficient grounds to justify the arrest, search, and seizure, unless the officer brings himself within one of the rules laid down authorizing an arrest without a warrant. Where a credible person gives information to a peace officer that a felony has been committed, not only the fact of the commission of the crime, but also the identity of the accused as well must be disclosed to the officer. 

In this case the officers had been requested to pick up a certain automobile of a designated license number, and nothing more. (115 S.W. 2d 923.)

Defendant was driving his automobile on the streets of the town of Nacogdoches at approximately thirty-five miles per hour, and was arrested by officers for speeding. The officers searched the defendant's car, and discovered twelve quarts of wine being transported in a dry area in violation of law for which the defendant was tried. The officers had a right to arrest the defendant when they observed him violating the speed law in their presence. Although they had no knowledge that the defendant was transporting intoxicating liquor, and although they did not arrest him for that offense, the officers had the right to search defendant's automobile as an incident to a legal arrest. (116 S.W. 2d 714.)

The defendant had stolen two diamond rings. When he and his companion attempted to get them appraised at a pawn shop, certain peace officers who had received information concerning the theft, apprehended the defendant at the pawn shop. The defendant placed the rings in his mouth and swallowed them.

The officers took the defendant to a hospital, and, through use of a fluoroscope, located the rings in the lower part of the defendant's bowels. Although the officers kept the defendant under watch for hours, he steadfastly refused to permit his bowls to move and pass the rings. Finally, the officers took him to another hospital and by administering an effective enema, caused him, contrary to his wishes, to eliminate the incriminating evidence, namely, the rings.

The question of law in this case involved the legality of the arrest and the novel search and seizure which followed. For an arrest without warrant and a search and seizure to be legal, all must come squarely within the statutes authorizing arrest and search. Here, an offense was being committed in the presence of the officer's; by swallowing the rings to hide them, the defendant was committing a felony for which they had a legal right to arrest him and to search his person. The same right which officers might have to cut the stitches in order to remove stolen property from the lining of defendant's coat, permitted them to remove the rings from defendant's person by means of an enema, which was the natural way of obtaining the evidence in view of the unusual manner in which the defendant concealed the rings. (141 S.W. 2d 341.)

The companion of the defendant in the above cited case was indicted for the crime of receiving and concealing stolen property. He was convicted on the strength of the evidence obtained by means of the enema. (143 S.W. 2d 617.)

In a case of unlawfully carrying a pistol, two officers saw the defendant park his automobile near a cafe, and as they approached the defendant turned around and ducked down as if to put something under the front seat of the car. One of the officers walked around the automobile and shined his flashlight inside to look around. He then rejoined the other officer who was asking the defendant if he had a gun on him. The defendant replied, "No," whereupon the officer who had been inspecting the car, wheeled around and looked under the car seat and found a gun. Seeing that the officer was about to discover the weapon, the defendant admitted having a gun. 

The question of law involved in this case is whether or not the officers had a legal right to search the automobile, and if not, was the defendant's admission that he had a gun sufficient to prove his guilt, and did he consent to the search of his automobile. There was no request made to search the car and no permission was expressly given. The admission of the defendant that he had a gun was not sufficient proof of his guilt. Before the defendant could be convicted of unlawfully carrying a firearm, it was necessary to search the car; hence the proof of guilt depended upon the legality of the search. Since no offense was being committed in the presence of the officers which would have authorized an arrest without warrant, and since there was not enough probable cause to warrant and authorize a search of the automobile, the search was illegal. No conviction can be had solely upon evidence obtained through an illegal search. (235 S.W. 2d 183.)

The police, suspecting the defendant of operating an illegal lottery, kept the rooming house where he lived under surveillance for two months. At one time during the watch, the officers heard the sound of an adding machine coming from the defendant's room, an indication that he was making computations for his numbers racket. This was the kind of evidence needed and one of the few times it would be available. The officers entered the house through the landlady's bedroom, unannounced, uninvited, and without a search warrant. They proceeded down the hallway to the defendant's room. The door was closed but one of the officers climbed on a chair and by looking through the transom, witnessed the defendant engaged in what appeared to be the lottery procedure. The defendant was arrested, his quarters were searched, and evidence gathered. 

The court held the search and seizure to be illegal. No emergency existed and the officers, who had been waiting for two months, never secured a search warrant. They illegally entered the rooming house through the landlady's bedroom, hence they were illegally in the hallway when they witnessed the defendant's activity. Had the officers entered the building in a lawful manner, the outcome of their search might have been different. (335 

U.S. 451.)

Soon after finding the body of a man who had apparently been murdered, the deputy sheriff filed an affidavit before a justice of the peace alleging that implements used in connection with the murder were kept and concealed in the home of the defendant. 

A search warrant was issued and the officer proceeded to search the house. He did not find the implements used in connection with the murder, but he did find clothing on which were spots, later identified as human blood. Grease spots were also found on the clothes and hair similar to that of the murder victim. 

The fruit of this search was not admissible in the trial of the defendant. The search for implements was authorized but no search was authorized for purely evidentiary matters such as the clothing. (197 S.W. 2d 570.)

A constable arrested a truck driver on the highway when he refused to drive his vehicle approximately two miles back to the city of Grandview to be weighed. The arrest was illegal because under Art. 827a-6 P.C., only weight inspectors of the State 

Highway Department have the authority to require that vehicles be weighed, and that the driver unload any excess weight. The constable did not have the proper authority, and the driver was not guilty of any breach of the peace in the officer's presence,

hence the refusal of the driver in no way warranted an arrest. (96 S.W. 2d 981.)

The defendant was a porter on a train and was walking home from work one evening carrying a large canvas bag when he was stopped by a police officer. The officer arrested the man and upon searching the bag, found some $20.00 worth of linens which belonged to the railroad company.

The arrest and search were held to be illegal. The defendant had not committed a felony or breach of the peace in the officer's presence; furthermore, the arrest of a person on mere suspicion without a warrant, and a search of his person or his premises without a search warrant, are not authorized by Art. 212, 213, and 325 C.C.P. To prevent the consequences of theft an officer may arrest without warrant and recover the property; however, to justify a seizure there must be reasonable grounds to suppose the property stolen. A "hunch" that turns out to be correct will not justify an unauthorized search and seizure. (215 S.W. 2d 180.)

The defendant entered a grocery store and attempted to get a check cashed. An employee was about to cash the check when the store owners advised him not to do so. The check was returned to the defendant who left the store and went into a nearby hotel. The sheriff was notified by the store employee that somebody was trying to cash a check; the employee was suspicious that something was wrong in the matter. The sheriff went to the hotel and found the defendant hiding under the bed. The sheriff arrested him and while searching his hotel room, found several scraps of paper in the commode, which, when pieced together, turned out to be a forged check. 

The arrest, search, and seizure were illegal. The sheriff had no warrant of any kind nor had he been informed by a credible person that a felony had been committed and that the offender was about to escape. The store employee had only a mere suspicion that something might be wrong with the check; he would have cashed the check had the store owner permitted him, for it looked in all respects to be genuine. Moreover, even if the employee had informed the sheriff that a felony had been committed, it was necessary that the offender be about to escape and that there was no time to procure a warrant. The defendant was not escaping, but had registered in a hotel nearby. The same right which a person has under the Constitution, to be safe from unreasonable searches of his home, applies equally to a person's hotel room. The arrest and search by the sheriff were illegal and the evidence found was not admissible against the defendant,

es 2 Ect ​wer fend spic wal he even though the check proved the defendant was guilty of a crime. (267 S.W. 2d 410.)

Police officers in the city of Fort Worth were informed by a woman that some man had committed an assault and battery upon her, and that he was staying at a certain hotel. The officers proceeded to the hotel, and with the assistance of a bellboy, obtained entrance to the defendant's room, whereupon they found him in bed. In resisting arrest by the officers, the defendant planted a blow in the groin of one of the officers.

The arrest was illegal and the defendant was not guilty of an assault and battery on the officer for attempting to extricate himself from an illegal arrest. The officers had been informed of the commission of a misdemeanor, and upon this information and nothing more, they invaded the defendant's hotel room and arrested him.

Officers went to the private residence of the defendant for the purpose of locating some person other than the defendant. 

When they arrived they discovered the defendant attempting to dispose of liquor which defendant possessed in violation of law. 

The arrest of the defendant and seizure of the liquor were illegal because the officers had no warrant of any kind, nor did they have a probable cause for believing the defendant possessed illegal liquor.

SECTION 2-OTHER WRITS AND PROCESS Art. 113 C.C.P. Definition. 

HABEAS CORPUS 

The writ of habeas corpus is the remedy to be used when any person is restrained in his liberty. It is an order issued by a court or judge of competent jurisdiction, directed to anyone having a person in his custody, or under his restraint, commanding him to produce such person, at a time and place named in the writ, and show why he is held in custody or under restraint. 

The object of the writ of habeas corpus is to relieve a person from illegal restraint as quickly as possible. (210 S.W. 2d 976.)

Art. 114 C.C.P. To Whom Directed. 

The writ runs in the name of "The State of Texas." It is addressed to a person having another under restraint, or in his custody, describing, as near as may be, the name of the office, if any, of the person to whom it is directed, and the name of the person said to be detained. It shall fix the time and place of reer

turn, and be signed by the judge, or by the clerk with his seal, where issued by a court.

Art. 117 C.C.P. By Whom Writ May Be Granted. 

The Court of Criminal Appeals, the district courts, the county courts, or any judge of said courts, have power to issue the writ of habeas corpus; and it is their duty, upon proper application, to grant the writ under the rules prescribed by law. 

Art. 126 C.C.P. Requisites of Petition. 

The petition must state substantially: 

1. That the person for whose benefit the application is made is illegally restrained in his liberty, and by whom-naming both parties, if their names are known, or, if unknown, designating and describing them. 

2. When the party is confined or restrained by virtue of any writ, order or process, or under color of either, a copy shall be annexed to the petition, or it shall be stated that a copy cannot be obtained. 

3. When the confinement or restraint is not by virtue of any writ, order or process, the petition may state only that the party is illegally confined or restrained in his liberty. 

4. There must be a prayer in the petition for the writ of habeas corpus. 

5. Oath must be made that the allegations of the petition are true, according to the belief of the petitioner. 

Art. 129 C.C.P. Judge May Issue Warrant of Arrest. 

Whenever it appears by satisfactory evidence to any judge authorized to issue such writ that anyone is held in illegal confinement or custody, and there is good reason to believe that he will be carried out of the state, or suffer some irreparable injury before he can obtain relief in the usual course of law, or whenever the writ of habeas corpus has been issued and disregarded, the said judge may issue a warrant to any peace officer, or to any person specially named by said judge, directing him to take and bring such person before such judge, to be dealt with according to law. 

Art. 131 C.C.P. Proceedings Under the Warrant. 

The officer charged with the execution of the warrant shall bring the persons therein mentioned before the judge or court issuing the same, who shall inquire into the cause of the imprisonment or restraint, and make an order thereon, as in case of habeas corpus, either remanding into custody, discharging or admitting to bail the party so imprisoned or restrained.

Art. 135 C.C.P. Scope of Writ. 

The writ of habeas corpus is intended to be applicable to all such cases of confinement and restraint, where there is no lawful right in the person exercising the power, or where, though the power in fact exists, it is exercised in a manner or degree not sanctioned by law.

Art. 137 C.C.P. Person Afflicted with Disease. 

When a judge or court authorized to grant writs of habeas corpus shall be satisfied, upon investigation, that a person in legal custody is afflicted with a disease which will render a removal necessary for the preservation of life, an order may be made for the removal of the prisoner to some other place where his health will not be likely to suffer; or he may be admitted to bail when it appears that any species of confinement will endanger his life.

Art. 139 C.C.P. Service and Return. 

The writ may be served by delivering a copy of the original to the person who is charged with having the party under restraint or in custody, and exhibiting the original, if demanded; if he refuse to receive it, he shall be informed verbally of the purport of the writ. If he refuses admittance to the person wishing to make the service, or conceals himself, a copy of the writ may be fixed upon some conspicuous part of the house where such person resides or conceals himself, or of the place where the prisoner is confined; and the person serving the writ of habeas corpus shall, in all cases, state fully in his return, the manner and the time of the service of the writ.

The service of the writ may be made by any person competent to testify. (Art. 138 C.C.P.) The return of the writ, if made by any person other than an officer, shall be under oath. (Art. 

140 C.C.P.)

Art. 141 C.C.P. Must Make Return. 

The person on whom the writ of habeas corpus is served shall immediately obey the same, and make the return required by law upon the copy of the original writ served on him, and this, whether the writ be directed to him or not. 

Art. 142 C.C.P. How Return Is Made. 

The return is made by stating in plain language upon the copy of the writ or some paper connected with it: 

1. Whether it is true or not, according to the statement of the petition, that he has in his custody, or under his restraint, the person named or described in such petition. 

2. By virtue of what authority, or for what cause, he took and detains such person.

3. If he had such person in his custody or under restraint at any time before the service of the writ, and has transferred him to the custody of another, he shall state particularly to whom, at what time, for what reason or by what authority he made such transfer. 

4. He shall annex to his return the writ or warrant, if any, by virtue of which he holds the person in custody. 

5. The return must be signed and sworn to by the person making it.

Art. 143 C.C.P. Applicant Brought Before Judge. 

The person on whom the writ is served shall bring before the judge the person in his custody, or under his restraint, unless it be made to appear that by reason of sickness he cannot be removed; in which case, another day may be appointed by the judge or court for hearing the cause, and for the production of the person confined; or the application may be heard and decided without the production of the person detained, by the consent of his counsel.

Art. 172 C.C.P. Refusing to Execute Writ. 

Any officer to whom a writ of habeas corpus, or other writ, warrant or process authorized by this chapter shall be directed, delivered or tendered, who refuses to execute the same according to his directions, or who wantonly delays the service or execution of the same shall be liable to fine as for contempt of court.

Art. 174 C.C.P. Refusal to Give Copy of Process. 

Any jailer, sheriff or other officer who has a prisoner in his custody and refuses, upon demand, to furnish a copy of the process under which he holds the person, is guilty of an offense. 

Art. 441 C.C.P. Definition. 

CAPIAS 

A capias is a writ issued by the court or clerk, and directed "To any sheriff of the State of Texas," commanding him to arrest a person accused of an offense and bring him before that court forthwith, or on a day or at a term stated in the writ. 

Art. 442 C.C.P. Requisites of Capias. 

A capias shall be held sufficient if it have the following requisites: 

1. That it run in the name of the "State of Texas." 

2. That it name the person whose arrest is ordered or if unknown, describe him.

3. That it specify the offense of which the defendant is accused, and it appear thereby that he is accused of some offense against the penal law of the state. 

4. That it name the court to which and the time when it is returnable. 

5. That it be dated and attested officially by the authority issuing the same. 

Art. 447 C.C.P. Capias Does Not Lose Its Force. 

A capias shall not lose its force if not executed and returned at the time fixed in the writ, but may be executed at any time afterward, and return made. All proceedings under such capias shall be as valid as if the same had been executed and returned within the time specified in the writ. 

Art. 448 C.C.P. Reasons for Retaining Capias. 

When the capias is not returned at the time fixed in the writ, the officer holding it shall notify the court from whence it was issued, in writing, of his reasons for retaining it. 

Art. 453 C.C.P. Who May Arrest Under Capias. 

A capias may be executed by any constable or other peace officer. In felony cases, the defendant must be delivered forthwith to the sheriff of the county where the arrest is made, together with the writ under which he was taken. 

Art. 457 C.C.P. Return of Bail Bond and Capias. 

When an arrest has been made and a bail bond taken, such bond, together with the capias, shall be returned forthwith to the proper court.

Art. 460 C.C.P. Return of Capias. 

The return of the capias shall be made to the court from which it is issued. If it has been executed, the return shall state what disposition has been made of the defendant. If it has not been executed, the cause of the failure to execute it shall be fully stated. If the defendant has not been found, the return shall further show what efforts have been made by the officer to find him, and what information he has as to the defendant's whereabouts.

In cases of arrest for felony in the county where the prosecution is pending, during a term of court, the officer making the arrest may take bail. (Art. 450 C.C.P.) The court shall fix the amount of bail and the sheriff, or other peace officer, unless it be the police of a city, is authorized to take a bail bond of the accused in the amount as fixed by the court, to be approved by such officer taking the same, and will thereupon discharge

the accused from custody. It shall not be necessary for the defendant or his sureties to appear in court. (Art. 287 C.C.P.) 

Art. 451 C.C.P. Sheriff May Take Bail in Felony. 

In cases of arrest for felony less than capital, made during vacation or made in another county than the one in which the prosecution is pending, the sheriff may take bail; in such cases, the amount of the bail shall be the same as is endorsed upon the capias; and if no amount be endorsed on the capias, the sheriff shall require a reasonable amount of bail. 

The sheriff's department has no authority to hold a person charged with a felony or a misdemeanor in jail for any length of time for the purpose of making an investigation or for other reasons without giving the accused an opportunity to make bail. (Op.Atty.Gen. 1944, No. 6136.) The sheriff of the county from which the venue has been changed cannot accept bail for the defendant in his custody or dispose of him otherwise than by delivery to the sheriff of the county acquiring jurisdiction. (44 

S.W. 1110.) Where, for example, a person is arrested and imprisoned in Dallas County on capias issued out of Val Verde County, wherein he has no friends to sign bond, is entitled to bail in Dallas County, conditioned that the prisoner return to 

Val Verde County to answer the charge. (79 S.W. 2d 1097.)

Art. 454 C.C.P. Bail in Misdemeanor. 

Any officer making an arrest under a capias in a misdemeanor may in term time or vacation take bail of the defendant.

Art. 455 C.C.P. Arrested in Capital Case. 

Where an arrest is made under a capias in a capital case, the sheriff shall confine the defendant in jail, and the capias shall, for that purpose, be sufficient commitment. This article is applicable when the arrest is made in the county where the prosecution is pending.

Art. 456 C.C.P. Arrest in Capital Case in Another County. 

In each capital case where a defendant is arrested under a capias in a county other than that in which the case is pending, the sheriff who arrests or to whom the defendant is delivered, shall convey him forthwith to the county from which the capias issued and deliver him to the sheriff of such county. 

When a judgment has been rendered against a defendant for a pecuniary fine, if he is present, he shall be imprisoned in jail until discharged as provided by law. (Art. 787 C.C.P.) When a pecuniary fine has been adjudged against a defendant not present, a capias shall forthwith be issued for his arrest. The sheriff shall execute the same by placing the defendant in jail. (Art. 

788 C.C.P.)

Art. 789 C.C.P. Capias Shall Recite. 

Where such capias issues, it shall state the rendition and amount of the judgment and the amount unpaid thereon, and command the sheriff to take the defendant and place him in jail until the amount due upon such judgment and the further costs of collecting the same are paid, or until the defendant is otherwise legally discharged.

Art. 790 C.C.P. Capias May Issue to Any County. 

The capias provided for above may be issued to any county in the state, and shall be executed and returned as in other counties, but no bail shall be taken in such cases. 

Art. 796 C.C.P. Capias for Imprisonment. 

A capias issued for the arrest and commitment of one convicted of a misdemeanor, the penalty of which or any part thereof is imprisonment in jail, shall recite the judgment and command the sheriff to place the defendant in jail, to remain the length of time therein fixed; and this writ shall be sufficient to authorize the sheriff to place such defendant in jail. 

Where a defendant has been convicted of any felony or any grade of theft while under a suspended sentence for a previous conviction, a capias will issue for his arrest. (Art. 779 C.C.P.) 

Where a judgment in a felony case has been affirmed by the Court of Criminal Appeals, and the defendant is out on bail, a capias shall issue for his arrest to serve sentence. (Art. 819 

C.C.P.) Capias will also issue for a defendant where a misdemeanor case has been affirmed against him, Art. 851 C.C.P.); and capias is the procedure for arresting the defendant to satisfy judgment in the justice court, (Art. 918 C.C.P.; and in the corporation court, (Art. 870 C.C.P.).

Art. 461 C.C.P. Definition. 

SUBPOENA 

A subpoena is a writ issued to the sheriff or other proper officer commanding him to summon one or more persons therein named to appear at a certain term of the court, or on a certain day, to testify in a criminal action, or before an examining court, coroner's inquest, the grand jury, or before a judge hearing an application under habeas corpus, or in any other case in which the testimony of a witness may be required under the provisions of this Code. The writ shall be dated and signed officially by the court or clerk issuing the same, but need not be under seal. 

Art. 462 C.C.P. Subpoena Duces Tecum. 

If a witness have in his possession any instrument of writing or other thing desired as evidence, the subpoena may specify

such evidence and direct that the witness bring the same with him and produce it in court. 

Art. 464 C.C.P. Service and Return of Subpoena. 

A subpoena is served by reading the same in the hearing of the witness. The officer having the subpoena shall make due return thereof, showing the time and manner of service, if served, and, if not served, he shall show in his return the cause of his failure to serve it; and, if the witness could not be found, he shall state the diligence he has used to find him, and what information he has as to the whereabouts of the witness. 

A subpoena must be read to the witness in person and cannot be served by reading over a telephone. (95 S.W. 536.) Where the subpoena contains the names of several witnesses, the return should indicate which witnesses were served and which were not. (5 Tex.Cr.R. 163.) If a witness refuse to obey a subpoena, he may be fined at the discretion of the court. (Art. 

456 C.C.P.)

Art. 466 C.C.P. What Is Disobedience of a Subpoena. 

It shall be held that a witness refuses to obey a subpoena : 

1. If he is not in attendance on the court on the day set apart for taking up the criminal docket or on any day subsequent thereto and before the final disposition or continuance of the particular case in which he is a witness. 

2. If he is not in attendance at any other time named in a writ. 

3. If he refuses without legal cause to produce evidence in his possession which he has been summoned to bring with him and produce.

Art. 476 C.C.P. Duty of Officer Receiving Subpoena. 

The officer receiving said subpoena shall execute the same by delivering a copy thereof to each witness therein named. He shall make due return of said subpoena, showing therein the time and manner of executing the same, and, if not executed, such return shall show why not executed, the diligence used to find said witness, and such information as the officer has as to the whereabouts of said witness. 

Art. 477 C.C.P. Subpoena Returnable Forthwith. 

When a subpoena is returnable forthwith, the officer shall immediately serve the witness with a copy of the same; and it shall be the duty of said witness to immediately make his appearance before the court, magistrate or other authority issuing the same. If said witness makes affidavit of his inability from lack of funds to appear in obedience to said subpoena, the officer executing the same shall provide said witness, if said

subpoena be issued in a felony case, with the necessary funds or means to appear in obedience to said subpoena, taking his receipt therefor, and showing in his return on said subpoena, under oath, the amount furnished to said witness, together with the amount of his fees for executing said subpoena. 

When the subpoena is returned, a certificate is issued to the officer for the amount furnished the witness, together with the amount of fees the officer is entitled to, and the state then makes payment. (Art. 478 C.C.P.) 

If the subpoena is returnable at some future date, the officer shall have authority to take a good bail bond of the witness for his appearance under the subpoena. The bond shall be returned with the subpoena and shall be made payable to the State of Texas. If the witness refuses to give bond, he shall be kept in custody until such time as he starts in obedience to the subpoena, when he shall be, upon affidavit being made, provided with funds necessary to appear in obedience to the subpoena. (Art. 479 C.C.P.) 

ATTACHMENT OF WITNESS Art. 471 C.C.P. Definition. 

An attachment is a writ issued by a clerk of a court under seal, or by any magistrate, or by the foreman of a grand jury, in any criminal action or proceeding authorized by law, commanding some peace officer to take the body of a witness and bring him before such court, magistrate or grand jury on a day named, or forthwith, to testify in behalf of the state or of the defendant, as the case may be. It shall be dated and signed officially by the officer issuing it. 

Art. 472 C.C.P. When Attachment May Issue. 

When a witness who resides in the county of the prosecution has been duly served with a subpoena to appear and testify in any criminal action or proceeding fails to so appear, the state or the defendant shall be entitled to have an attachment issued forthwith for such witness.

Art. 473 C.C.P. Attachment for Resident Witness. 

When a witness resides in the county of the prosecution, whether he has disobeyed a subpoena or not, either in term time or vacation, upon the filing of an affidavit with the clerk by the defendant or state's counsel, that he has good reason to believe, and does believe, that such witness is a material witness, and is about to move out of the county, the clerk shall forthwith issue an attachment for such witness; provided, that in misdemeanor cases, when the witness makes oath that he cannot give surety, the officer executing the attachment shall take his personal bond.

A witness may be attached in order that he appear before a grand jury. (Art. 382 and Art. 474 C.C.P.) A witness may be attached where he resides out of the county in which the grand jury is in session or the prosecution is pending. (Art. 383 and 

Art. 475 C.C.P.) A justice of the peace may issue a subpoena or writ of attachment to secure attendance of witnesses before an inquest. (Art. 974 C.C.P.) 

Texas has adopted the Uniform Act to Secure Attendance of Witnesses from Without the State, incorporating its provisions into Art. 486a C.C.P. The Act provides for the summoning of persons from another state to appear in Texas as a material witness; and it provides for the commanding of persons in Texas to attend and testify in proceedings in other states. Witnesses summoned under the provisions of Art. 486a C.C.P. are provided immunity from arrest and service of process, civil or criminal, in connection with matters which arose before the entrance of the witness into the state under the summons. The object of this 

Act is to provide for the enforcement of the criminal laws and administration of justice in criminal proceedings in the several states by obtaining the testimony of witnesses who are outside the state in which the proceedings are being conducted. 

INDICTMENT 

The primary pleading in a criminal action on the part of the state is the indictment or information. (Art. 504 C.C.P.) An indictment is an accusation in writing found and presented by a grand jury, legally convoked and sworn, to the court in which it is impaneled, charging that a person therein named has done some act, or been guilty of some omission, which, by law, is a public offense, punishable on indictment. (216 S.W. 1086.)

Art. 487 C.C.P. Service of Indictment in Felony Case. 

In every case of felony, when the accused is in custody, or as soon as he may be arrested, the clerk of the court where an indictment has been presented shall immediately make a certified copy of the same, and deliver such copy to the sheriff, together with a writ directed to such sheriff, commanding him forthwith to deliver such certified copy to the accused. 

Art. 488 C.C.P. Service and Return. 

Upon receipt of such writ and copy, the sheriff shall immediately deliver such certified copy of the indictment to the accused and return the writ to the clerk issuing the same, with his return thereon, showing when and how the same was executed.

SECTION 3-LIMITATION ON CRIMINAL ACTIONS 

Limitation in criminal law means that, after the expiration of the time specified in the statutes, no criminal action may be brought against a person accused of the particular crime involved. When the state brings an action against a person, it is the duty of the state to show the time of the offense in order to prove that it occurred within the period limiting the time for the bringing of criminal actions. Failure to prove the time of the commission of the offense is fatal to a conviction. (8 S.W. 477.)

The period of limitation is computed from the time the offense is committed until the indictment or information is presented; however, the day on which the offense is committed, and the day on which the indictment or information is presented, are excluded from the computation. (Art. 182 C.C.P.) An indictment is considered as "presented," when it has been duly acted upon by the grand jury and received by the court. (Art. 

184 C.C.P.) An information is considered as "presented" when it has been filed by the proper officer in the proper court. (Art. 

185 C.C.P.)

Art. 183 C.C.P. sets out further rules for the computation of the period of limitation: 

1. The time during which the accused is absent from the state is not computed in the period of limitation. 

2. The time during the pendency of an indictment, information, or complaint is not computed in the period of limitation. 

3. The term "during the pendency," as used above, means that period of time beginning with the day the indictment, information, or complaint is filed in a court of competent jurisdiction and ending with the day such accusation is, by an order of a trial court having jurisdiction thereof, determined to be invalid for any reason.

The period of limitation for the various crimes is the follow

Murder Treason No Limit 20 years 

Conversion of an estate 10 years Forgery 10 years Uttering, using, or passing forged instrument 10 years Arson 5 years Burglary 5 years

Counterfeiting 

Felony theft Robbery 

Other felonies Misdemeanors

Rape 

5 years 

5 years 

5 years 

3 years 

2 years 1 year

PART III  CHAPTER 9 

Ancillary Writs

SECTION 1 — IN GENERAL INTRODUCTION 

Ancillary writs are civil process which aid in the disposition of a main proceeding. The principal ancillary writs are: Attachment, Execution, Garnishment, and Sequestration. These writs are ordinarily utilized to seize property.

The writ of attachment is a writ issued after a main suit is filed for the purpose of bringing enough of the defendant's property into the custody of the court to satisfy the plaintiff's demand in the event he is successful in establishing his claim against the defendant.

The writ of execution is a writ issued after the final judgment in a civil proceeding for the purpose of putting into effect the final decree of the court. By virtue of this writ, property of the defendant is levied upon to satisfy the judgment rendered in favor of the plaintiff and against the defendant. 

The writ of sequestration is a writ issued after a main suit is filed for the purpose of bringing certain property, which is in controversy, into the custody of the court. The property seized by sequestration is a specific piece of property in which the plaintiff is asserting some right, title or interest. 

The writ of garnishment is a writ issued after a main suit is filed for the purpose of seizing any goods, moneys or credits of a debtor which are under the control of some third party. Where the plaintiff is unable to attach any property of the defendant he may obtain a writ of garnishment and seize any goods, moneys or credits of the defendant while they are in the hands of some third party, and thus the plaintiff may satisfy his demand somewhat indirectly.

No civil suit shall be commenced nor process issued or served on Sunday, except in cases of injunction, attachment, garnishment, sequestration, or distress proceedings. (Rule 6 T.C.P.)

In computing any period of time prescribed by the Rules of Civil Procedure, by order of court, or by any applicable statute, the day of the act, event, or default after which the designated period of time begins to run is not to be included. The last day of the period so computed is to be included, unless it is a Sunday or a legal holiday, in which event the period runs until the end of the next day which is neither a Sunday nor a holiday. (Rule 4 T.C.P.)

DUTIES OF OFFICERS RECEIVING WRITS Art. 6873 R.C.S. Sheriffs to Execute Process and Other Writs. 

Each sheriff shall execute all process and precepts directed to him by legal authority, and make return thereof to the proper court, on or before the day to which the same is returnable; and any sheriff who shall fail so to do, or who shall make a false return on any process or precept shall, for every such offense, be liable to be fined by the court to which such process is returnable, as for a contempt, not exceeding $100.00 at the discretion of the court, which fine shall go to the county treasury; and such sheriff shall also be liable to the party injured for all damages he may sustain.

Art. 6885 R.C.S. Duties of Constable in General. 

Each constable shall execute and return according to law all process, warrants and precepts to him directed and delivered by any lawful officer, attended upon all justice courts held in his precinct and perform all such other duties as may be required of him by law.

Art. 6887 R.C.S. Failure to Execute or Return Process and Other Writs. 

If any constable shall fail or refuse to execute and return, according to law, any process, warrant, or precept to him lawfully directed and delivered, he shall be fined for a contempt, on motion of the party injured, before the court from which such process, warrant, or precept issued, not less than $10.00 nor more than $100.00, with costs; which fine shall be for the benefit of the party injured. Said constable shall have ten days' notice of such motion.

Art. 6889 R.C.S. Jurisdiction. 

Every constable may execute any process, civil or criminal, throughout his county and elsewhere, as may be provided for in the Code of Criminal Procedure, or other law. 

Art. 6877 R.C.S. Unfinished Business of Sheriff or Constable. 

When any sheriff or any constable shall from any cause vacate his office, all unfinished business whatsoever in his hands.

ANCILLARY WRITS 

187 shall be transferred to his successor, and be completed by him. in the same manner as if commenced by himself. 

SECTION 2-WRIT OF ATTACHMENT DEFINITION 

A writ of attachment is a process for seizing persons or property and bringing them into the custody of the law. The purpose of the writ is to take a defendant's property into legal custody, so that it may be applied on the defendant's debt to the plaintiff when established. (137 S.W. 2d 344.)

REQUISITES OF WRIT OF ATTACHMENT 

Rule 593 T.C.P. To Whom Directed.

The writ of attachment shall be directed as follows: "To the 

Sheriff or Any Constable of Any County of the State of Texas."

It shall command him to attach so much of the property of the defendant as shall be found within his county, sufficient to satisfy the demand of the plaintiff and the probable costs of the suit.

Rule 594 T.C.P. Form of Writ. 

The following form of writ may be issued:

The State of Texas 

To the Sheriff or Any Constable of Any County of the 

State of Texas, Greeting: 

We command you that you attach forthwith so much of the property of C.D., if it be found in your county, repleviable on security, as shall be of value sufficient to make the sum of dollars, and the probable costs of suit, to satisfy the demand of A.B., and that you keep and secure in your hands the property so attached, unless replevied, that the same may be liable to further proceedings thereon to be had before our court in 

County of 

You will true return make of this writ on or before 10 a.m. of 

Monday, the day of how you have executed the same.

Rule 596 T.C.P. Delivery of Writ. 

19 ? showing The writ of attachment shall be dated and tested as other writs, and may be delivered to the sheriff or constable by the officer issuing it, or he may deliver it to the plaintiff, his agent or attorney, for that purpose.

DUTY OF OFFICER RECEIVING WRIT Rule 597 T.C.P. Duty of Officer. 

The sheriff or constable receiving the writ shall immediately proceed to execute the same by levying upon so much of the property of the defendant subject to the writ, and found within his county, as may be sufficient to satisfy the command of the writ. 

Rule 598 T.C.P. Levy, How Made. 

The writ of attachment shall be levied in the same manner as is, or may be, the writ of execution upon similar property. 

Art. 287 R.C.S. May Demand Indemnity. 

Whenever an officer shall levy an attachment, it shall be at his own risk. Such officer may, for his own indemnification, require the plaintiff in attachment to execute and deliver to him a bond of indemnity to secure him if it should afterward appear that the property levied upon by him does not belong to the defendant.

Art. 288 R.C.S. Property Subject to Attachment. 

The writ of attachment may be levied upon such property, and none other, as is, or may be, by law subject to levy under the writ of execution.

Art. 290 R.C.S. Attachment of Personal Property. 

When personal property is attached, the same shall remain in the hands of the officer attaching until final judgment, unless a claim be made thereto and bond be given to try the right to the same, or unless the same be replevied or be sold as provided by law.

Art. 6662 R.C.S. Attachments on Real Estate Recorded. 

Whenever an attachment is levied upon real estate, the officer levying the writ shall immediately file with the county clerk of the county or counties in which the real estate so levied upon is situated, a copy of the writ, together with a copy of so much of his return as relates to the land in said county. 

RIGHT TO REPLEVY Art. 291 R.C.S. Claimant's Bond and Affidavit. 

Any person other than the defendant may claim the personal property so levied on, or any part thereof, upon making the affidavit and giving bond required by the provisions of the title relating to the trial of the right of property. 

Art. 599 T.C.P. Defendant May Replevy Property. 

At any time before judgment, should the property not have

ANCILLARY WRITS 

189 been previously claimed or sold, the defendant may replevy the same, or any part thereof, by giving bond, with two or more good and sufficient sureties, to be approved by the officer who levied the writ, payable to the plaintiff, in double the amount of the plaintiff's debt, or, at the defendant's option, for the value of the property replevied, to be estimated by the officer, conditioned that should the defendant be condemned in the action, he shall satisfy the judgment which may be rendered against him therein, or shall pay the estimated value of the property with lawful interest thereon from the date of the bond. 

EFFECT OF WRIT Art. 300 R.C.S. Attachment Creates a Lien. 

The execution of the writ of attachment upon any property of the defendant subject thereto, unless the writ should be quashed or otherwise vacated, shall create a lien from the date of such levy on the real estate levied on, and on such personal property as remains in the hands of the attaching officer, and on the proceeds of such personal property as may have been sold.

SALE OF PERISHABLE PROPERTY 

Whenever personal property shall be made to appear that it is in danger of serious and immediate waste or decay, or deterioration in value if kept until the time of trial, the judge may order the property to be sold. (Rule 600 T.C.P.) If the property is found to be perishable, the judge may order the sheriff or constable to sell the property at public auction for cash, and thereupon the officer shall sell it accordingly. (Rule 

601 T.C.P.) The sale of attached perishable personal property shall be conducted in the same manner as sales of personal property under execution. (Rule 603 T.C.P.)

The officer making a sale of perishable property shall promptly pay the proceeds of such sale to the clerk of such court or justice of the peace, and shall make written return of the order of sale signed by him officially, stating the time and place of the sale, the name of the purchaser, and the amount of money received, with an itemized account of the expenses attending the sale. Such return shall be filed with the papers of the case. (Rule 604 T.C.P.)

RETURN OF WRIT OF ATTACHMENT 

Rule 606 T.C.P. Return of Writ.

The officer executing the writ of attachment shall return the writ, with his action endorsed thereon, or attached thereto,

signed by him officially, to the court from which it issued, at or before 10 o'clock a.m. of the Monday next after the expiration of fifteen days from the date of issuance of the writ. Such return shall describe the property attached with sufficient certainty to identify it, and state when the same was attached, and whether any personal property attached remains still in his hands, and, if not, the disposition made of the same.. When property has been replevied he shall deliver the replevy bond to the clerk or justice of the peace to be filed with the papers of the cause. The return may be amended by leave of the court. (Rule 

609 T.C.P.)

Rule 607 T.C.P. Report of Disposition of Property. 

When the property levied on is claimed, replevied or sold, or otherwise disposed of after the writ has been returned, the officer having the custody of the same shall immediately make a report in writing, signed by him officially, to the clerk, or justice of the peace, as the case may be, showing such disposition of the property. Such report shall be filed among the papers of the cause.

SECTION 3 WRIT OF EXECUTION 

DEFINITION 

Execution is the process for carrying out the decree of the court, the putting into effect of the final judgment. The writ of execution is a civil proceeding for enforcement of a judgment against property. It is the final process whereby possession is obtained of anything recovered by the judgment. 

Rule 621 T.C.P. Enforcement of Judgment.

The judgments of the district, county, and justice courts shall be enforced by execution or other appropriate process.

Such execution or other process shall be returnable in thirty, sixty, or ninety days as requested by the plaintiff, his agent or attorney.

EXECUTION OF WRIT 

Rule 622 T.C.P. Execution.

An execution is a process of the court from which it is issued. 

The clerk of the district or county court or the justice of the peace, as the case may be, shall tax the costs in every case in which a final judgment has been rendered and shall issue execution to enforce such judgment and collect such costs. The execution and subsequent executions shall not be addressed to a particular county, but shall be addressed to any sheriff or any constable within the State of Texas.

ANCILLARY WRITS 

191 

REQUISITES OF WRIT OF EXECUTION 

Rule 629 T.C.P. Requisites.

The style of the execution shall be "The State of Texas." It shall be directed to any sheriff or any constable within the State of Texas. It shall be signed by the clerk or justice officially, and bear the seal of the court, if issued out of the district or county court, and shall require the officer to execute it according to its terms, and to make the costs which have been adjudged against the defendant in execution and the further costs of executing the writ. It shall describe the judgment, stating the court in which, and the time when, rendered, and the names of the parties in whose favor and against whom the judgment was rendered. A correct copy of the bill of costs taxed against the defendant in execution shall be attached to the writ. It shall require the officer to return it within thirty, sixty, or ninety days, as directed by the plaintiff or his attorney. 

Art. 2446 R.C.S. Execution in Justice Courts. 

Such execution or other process shall conform to the requirements of writs. It shall describe the judgment and shall require the sheriff or constable of the proper county to execute the same, according to its terms, whether the same be to make a sum of money, or to deliver personal property, or to deliver possession of real estate, or to do some other thing; and, if for money, it shall state the rate of interest; and it shall also require the officer to make the costs which may have been adjudged against the defendant in execution, and the further costs of executing the writ. A certified copy of the costs, taxed against the defendant in execution according to the fee book up to the issuance of the execution, shall be attached to the writ. 

Rule 630 T.C.P. Execution on Judgment for Money.

When an execution upon a judgment for a sum of money, or directing the payment simply of a sum of money, it must specify in the body thereof the sum recovered or directed to be paid and the sum actually due when it is issued and the rate of interest upon the sum due. It must require the officer to satisfy the judgment and costs out of the property of the judgment debtor subject to execution by law.

Rule 631 T.C.P. Execution for Sale of Particular Property. 

An execution issued upon a judgment for the sale of particular chattels or personal property or real estate, must particularly describe the property, and shall direct the officer to make the sale by previously giving the public notice of the time and place of sale required by law and these rules.

Rule 632 T.C.P. Execution for Delivery of Certain Property. 

An execution issued upon a judgment for the delivery of the possession of a chattel or personal property, or for the delivery of the possession of real property, shall particularly describe the property, and designate the party to whom the judgment awards the possession. The writ shall require the officer to deliver the possession of the property to the party entitled thereto.

Rule 633 T.C.P. Execution for Possession or Value of Personal Property. 

If the judgment be for the recovery of personal property or its value, the writ shall command the officer, in case a delivery thereof cannot be had, to levy and collect the value thereof for which the judgment was recovered, to be specified therein, out of any property of the party against whom judgment was rendered, liable to execution.

INDORSEMENT BY OFFICER 

Rule 636 T.C..P. Indorsements by Officer.

The officer receiving the execution shall indorse thereon the exact hour and day when he received it. If he receives more than one on the same day against the same person he shall number them as received.

On failure of the officer to indorse the execution as required by Rule 636, or in case of false indorsement, he and his sureties shall be liable on motion in the court from whence the execution is issued, three days' notice being given, to a judgment in favor of the plaintiff in execution for twenty per cent on the amount of the execution, together with such damages as the plaintiff in execution may have sustained by such failure or such false in dorsement. (Art. 3785 R.C.S.) Art. 3787 R.C.S. On Death of Officer. 

If the officer receiving an execution die or go out of office before the return of any execution, his successor, or other officer authorized to discharge the duties of the office in such case, shall proceed therein in the same manner that such officer should have done.

OFFICER MAKING LEVY 

Rule 637 T.C.P. Levy of Execution.

When an execution is delivered to an officer he shall proceed without delay to levy the same upon the property of the defendant found within his county not exempt from execution, unless otherwise directed by the plaintiff, his agent or attorney. The officer shall first call upon the defendant, if he can be found, or, if absent, upon his agent within the county, if known, to

ANCILLARY WRITS 

193 point out property to be levied upon, and the levy shall first be made upon the property designated by the defendant, or his agent. If in the opinion of the officer the property so designated will not sell for enough to satisfy the execution and costs of sale, he shall require an additional designation by the defendant. 

If no property be thus designated by the defendant, the officer shall levy the execution upon any property of the defendant subject to execution.

Rule 638 T.C.P. Property Not to Be Designated. 

A defendant in execution shall not point out property which he has sold, mortgaged or conveyed in trust, or property exempt from forced sale. 

Rule 639 T.C.P. Levy.

In order to make levy on real estate, it shall not be necessary for the officer to go upon the ground but it shall be sufficient for him to indorse such levy on the writ. Levy upon personal property is made by taking possession thereof, when the defendant in execution is entitled to the possession. Where the defendant in execution has an interest in personal property, but is not entitled to the possession thereof, a levy is made thereon by giving notice thereof to the person who is entitled to the possession, or one of them where there are several. 

Ordinarily an officer making a levy on personal property must take possession of it, but where a levy is made on personal property of great bulk, weight, and immobility, the officer may merely go on the premises and point out the property, assert some dominion over it, and forbid its removal by the person against whom the writ of execution has been issued. The officer must do some positive act in making the levy, an act which would constitute a trespass except for the immunity furnished the officer by the writ. (191 S.W. 2d 774.)

Where the debtor is not entitled to possession of the property, even though he is in possession of it, provided he has an interest in it at the time of the levy, the statutes do not authorize an actual seizure of the property, but the levy should be by "notice." 

Rule 640 T.C.P. Levy on Stock Running at Large.

A levy upon livestock running at large in a range, and which cannot be herded and penned without great inconvenience and expense, may be made by designating by reasonable estimate the number of animals and describing them by their marks and brands, or either; such levy shall be made in the presence of two or more credible persons, and notice thereof shall be given in writing to the owner or his herder or agent, if residing within the county and known to the officer.

Rule 641 T.C.P. Levy on Shares of Stock. 

A levy upon shares of stock of any corporation or joint stock company for which a certificate is outstanding is made by the officer seizing and taking possession of such certificate. 

Rule 642 T.C.P. Levy Upon Interest of Partner.

A levy upon the interest of a partner in partnership property is made by leaving a notice with one or more of the partners or with a clerk of the partnership.

Rule 643 T.C.P. Levy on Goods Pledged or Mortgaged. 

Goods and chattels pledged, assigned or mortgaged as security for any debt or contract, may be levied upon and sold on execution against the person making the pledge, assignment or mortgage subject thereto; and the purchaser shall be entitled to the possession when it is held by the pledgee, assignee or mortgagee, on complying with the conditions of the pledge, assignment or mortgage.

Art. 3799 R.C.S. Liability of Officer for Loss. 

The officer shall keep securely all personal property levied on by him for which no delivery bond has been given. If any injury or loss should result by his negligence to any party interested, he and his sureties shall be liable to pay the value of the property so lost or the amount of the injury sustained, and ten per cent thereon, to be recovered by the party injured on motion, three days' notice being given in the court from which the execution issued.

Art. 3800 R.C.S. Expenses of Keeping Property. 

The officer shall be authorized to retain out of the proceeds of personal property sold under execution all reasonable expenses incurred by him in making the levy and keeping the property. 

REPLEVY AND DELIVERY BOND 

Rule 644 T.C.P. May Give Delivery Bond.

Any personal property taken in execution may be returned to the defendant by the officer upon the delivery by the defendant to him of a bond, payable to the plaintiff, with two or more good and sufficient sureties, to be approved by the officer, conditioned that the property shall be delivered to the officer at the time and place named in the bond, to be sold according to law, or for the payment to the officer of a fair value thereof, which shall be stated in the bond.

Rule 645 T.C.P. Property May Be Sold by Defendant. 

Where property has been replevied, as provided in the pre-

ANCILLARY WRITS 

195 ​E 10 ட் ur. in- of nd 6.8 2.25 2 TO 25 to Dre =0 of to ceding rule, the defendant may sell or dispose of the same, paying the officer the stipulated value thereof. 

Rule 646 T.C.P. Forfeited Delivery Bond. 

In case of the nondelivery of the property according to the terms of the delivery bond, and nonpayment of the value thereof, the officer shall forthwith indorse the bond "Forfeited" and return the same to the clerk of the court or the justice of the peace from which the execution issued; whereupon, if the judgment remain unsatisfied in whole or in part, the clerk or justice shall issue execution against the principal debtor and the sureties on the bond for the amount due, not exceeding the stipulated value of the property, upon which execution on delivery bond shall be taken, which instruction shall be indorsed by the clerk or justice on the execution.

SALE OF PROPERTY SEIZED BY EXECUTION Art. 3804 R.C.S. Sale of Real Property. 

Real property taken by virtue of any execution shall be sold at public auction, at the courthouse door of the county, on the first Tuesday of the month, between the hours of ten o'clock a.m. and four o'clock p.m.

The statutes provide that city lots shall be sold separately if possible. (Art. 3806 R.C.S.) A defendant, whose rural lands are being sold, may present a plat and field notes to the officer holding the execution, so that the defendant's land may be sold in lots of not less than fifty acres, in the order designated by the defendant. Only enough lots need be sold to satisfy the amount due on the execution. (Art. 3807 R.C.S.)

Rule 647 T.C.P. Notice of Sale of Real Estate. 

The time and place of sale of real estate under execution, order of sale, or venditioni exponas, shall be advertised by the officer by having the notice thereof published in the English language once a week for three consecutive weeks preceding such sale, in some newspaper published in said county. The first of said publications shall appear not less than twenty days immediately preceding the day of sale. Said notice shall contain a statement of the authority by virtue of which the sale is to be made, the time of levy, and the time and place of sale; it shall also contain a brief description of the property to be sold, and shall give the number of acres, original survey, locality in the county, and the name by which the land is most generally known, but it shall not be necessary for it to contain field notes. 

Publishers of newspapers shall charge the legal rate of two cents per word for the first insertion of such publication and one

cent per word for such subsequent insertions, or such newspapers shall be entitled to charge for such publication at a rate equal to but not in excess of the published word or line rate of that newspaper for such class of advertising. If there be no newspaper published in the county, or none which will publish the notice of sale for the compensation herein fixed, the officer shall then post such notice in writing in three public places in the county, one of which shall be at the courthouse door of such county, for at least twenty days successively next before the day of sale. The officer making the levy shall give the defendant, or his attorney, written notice of such sale, either in person or by mail, which notice shall substantially conform to the foregoing requirements. 

Rule 648 T.C.P. "Courthouse Door" Defined.

By the term "courthouse door" of a county is meant either of the principal entrances to the house provided by the proper authority for the holding of the district court. If from any cause there is no such house, the door of the house where the district court was last held in that county shall be deemed to be the courthouse door. Where the courthouse, or house used by the court, has been destroyed by fire or other cause, and another has not been designated by the proper authority, the place where such house stood shall be deemed to be the courthouse door. 

Rule 649 T.C.P. Sale of Personal Property. 

Personal property levied on under execution shall be offered for sale on the premises where it is taken in execution, or at the courthouse door of the county, or at some other place if, owing to the nature of the property, it is more convenient to exhibit it to purchasers at such place. Personal property susceptible of being exhibited shall not be sold unless the same be present and subject to the view of those attending the sale, except shares of stock in joint stock or incorporated companies, and in cases where the defendant in execution has merely an interest without right to the exclusive possession in which case the interest of defendant may be sold and conveyed without the presence or delivery of the property.

When a levy is made upon livestock running at large on the range, it is not necessary that such stock, or any part thereof, be present at the place of sale, and the purchaser at such sale is authorized to gather and pen such stock and select therefrom the number purchased by him.

Rule 650 T.C.P. Notice of Sale of Personal Property. 

Previous notice of the time and place of the sale of any personal property levied on under execution shall be given by post-

ANCILLARY WRITS 

197 ing notice thereof for ten days successively immediately prior to the day of sale at the courthouse door of any county and at the place where the sale is to be made. 

Art. 3816 R.C.S. Conveyance to Purchaser. 

When a sale has been made and the terms thereof complied with, the officer shall execute and deliver to the purchaser a conveyance of all the right, title, interest and claim which the defendant in execution had in and to the property sold. 

Art. 3820 R.C.S. Officer Shall Not Purchase. 

If any officer or his deputy making sale of property on execution, shall, directly or indirectly, purchase the same, the sale shall be void.

Rule 651 T.C.P. When Execution Not Satisfied. 

When the property levied upon does not sell for enough to satisfy the execution, the officer shall proceed anew, as in the first instance, to make the residue. 

Rule 653 T.C.P. Resale of Property.

When the terms of the sale shall not be complied with by the bidder the levying officer shall proceed to sell the same property again on the same day, if there be sufficient time; but if not, he shall readvertise and sell the same as in the first instance.

RETURN OF WRIT OF EXECUTION Rule 654 T.C.P. Return of Execution. 

The levying officer shall make due return of the execution, in writing and signed by him officially, stating concisely what such officer has done in pursuance of the requirements of the writ and of the law. The return shall be filed with the clerk of the court or the justice of the peace as the case may be. The execution shall be returned forthwith if satisfied by the collection of the money or if ordered by the plaintiff or his attorney indorsed thereon.

A return date is stated on the writ of execution, and the process must be executed within the time specified; otherwise, it is of no force and the right of an officer, by virtue of the writ, to take and sell property ceases. (151 S.W. 843.)

Rule 655 T.C.P. Return of Execution by Mail. 

When an execution is placed in the hands of an officer of a county other than the one in which the judgment is rendered, return may be made by mail; but money cannot be thus sent except by direction of the party entitled to receive the same or his attorney of record.

When an officer has collected money on execution, he shall pay the same to the party entitled thereto at the earliest opportunity. If he fails to do so he may be liable for the amount due plus five per cent per month interest as damages, besides interest and costs. (Art. 3824 R.C.S.) Art. 3825 R.C.S. Failure to Levy or Sell. 

Should an officer fail or refuse to levy upon or sell any property subject to execution, when the same might have been done, he and his sureties shall be liable to the party entitled to receive the money collected on such execution for the full amount of the debt, interest and costs, to be recovered on motion before the court from which said execution issued, five days' previous notice thereof being given to said officer and his sureties.

Art. 3826 R.C.S. Failure to Return Execution. 

Should an officer neglect or refuse to return any execution as required by law, or should he make a false return thereon, he and his sureties shall be liable to the party entitled to receive the money collected on such execution for the full amount of the debt, interest and costs, to be recovered as provided in the preceding article.

Art. 3827 R.C.S. Surplus to Be Paid to Defendant. 

If, on the sale of property, more money is received than is sufficient to pay the amount of the execution or executions in the hands of the officer, the surplus shall be immediately paid over to the defendant, his agent or attorney.

SECTION 4-WRIT OF GARNISHMENT DEFINITION 

A writ of garnishment is a statutory proceeding whereby a person's property, money, or credits in possession or under the control of another are applied to payment of the former's debt to some third person by proper statutory process against the debtor and garnishee. (106 S.W. 2d 1039.) Through the writ of garnishment a debtor's goods, moneys or credits can be reached while they are in the possession of some third party who is owing to the debtor. (111 S.W. 2d 25.)

ISSUANCE OF WRIT OF GARNISHMENT Art. 4076 R.C.S. Who May Issue and When. 

The clerks of the district and county courts and justices of the peace may issue writs of garnishment, returnable to their

ANCILLARY WRITS 

199 respective courts, in the following cases: 

1. Where an original attachment has been issued. 

2. Where the plaintiff sues for a debt and makes affidavit that such debt is just, due and unpaid, and that the defendant has not within his knowledge property in his possession within this state, subject to execution, sufficient to satisfy such debt; and that the garnishment applied for is not sued out to injure either the defendant or the garnishee. 

3. Where the plaintiff has a valid, subsisting judgment and makes affidavit that the defendant has not, within his knowledge, property in his possession within this state, subject to execution, sufficient to satisfy such judgment. 

Art. 4077 R.C.S. Bond. 

In the case mentioned in subdivision two of the preceding article, the plaintiff shall execute a bond, with two or more good and sufficient sureties, to be approved by the officer issuing the writ, payable to the defendant in the suit, in double the amount of the debt claimed therein, conditioned that he will prosecute his suit to effect and pay all damages and costs that may be adjudged against him for wrongfully suing out such garnishment. 

Rule 661 T.C.P. Form of Writ. 

The following form of writ may be used:

The State of Texas To E.F., Garnishee, Greeting: 

Whereas, in the 

Court of

County, (if a justice court, state also the number of the precinct), in a certain cause wherein A.B. is plaintiff and C.D. is defendant, the plaintiff, claiming an indebtedness against the said C.D. of dollars, besides interest and cost of suit, has applied for a writ of garnishment against you, E.C.: therefore you are hereby commanded to be and appear before said court at in said county (if the writ is issued from the county or district court, here proceed: "at 

10 o'clock a.m. on the Monday next following the expiration of twenty days from the date of service hereof." If the writ is issued from a justice of the peace court, here proceed: "at or before 10 o'clock a.m. on the Monday next after the expiration of ten days from the date of service hereof." In either event, proceed as follows:) then and there to answer upon oath what, if anything, you are indebted to the said C.D., and were when this writ was served upon you, and what effects,

if any, of the said C.D. you have in your possession, and had when this writ was served, and what other persons, if any, within your knowledge, are indebted to the said C.D. or have effects belonging to him in their possession. Herein fail not, but make due answer as the law directs.

DUTY OF OFFICER RECEIVING WRIT OF GARNISHMENT Rule 662 T.C.P. Delivery of Writ. 

The writ of garnishment shall be dated and tested as other writs, and may be delivered to the sheriff or constable by the officer who issued it, or he may deliver it to the plaintiff, his agent or attorney, for that purpose.

Rule 663 T.C.P. Execution and Return of Writ. 

The sheriff or constable receiving the writ of garnishment shall immediately proceed to execute the same by delivering a copy thereof to the garnishee, and shall make return thereof as of other citations.

Where a writ of garnishment is served on a bank holding deposit of debtor, the writ may be delivered to a cashier, and this will be sufficient to bind the bank. (27 S.W. 897.) If the garnishee is an incorporated company the writ must be delivered to an authorized agent of such company. (23 S.W. 2d 312.) When the officer levies a writ of garnishment upon shares of stock he should actually seize the stock certificates. (199 S.W. 2d 792.)

The defendant may, before judgment, replevy anything seized by garnishment, if he gives bond for double the amount of plaintiff's debt. (Rule 664 T.C.P.)

EFFECT OF WRIT OF GARNISHMENT 

From and after the service of such writ of garnishment, it shall not be lawful for the garnishee to pay to the defendant any debt or to deliver to him any effects; nor shall the garnishee, if an incorporated or joint stock company in which the defendant is alleged to be the owner of shares or to have an interest, permit or recognize any sale or transfer of such shares or interest; and any such payment or delivery, sale or transfer, shall be void and of no effect as to so much of said debt, effect, shares, or interest as may be necessary to satisfy the plaintiff's demand. 

(Art. 4084 R.C.S.) 

The garnishee must make an answer under oath, and in writing, to the writ of garnishment, making true answers to the

ANCILLARY WRITS 

201 several matters inquired of in the writ. (Rule 665 T.C.P.) If it appears from the answer of the garnishee that he is not indebted to the defendant, nor has any effects belonging to the defendant, and the answers of the garnishee are not controverted, as provided in Rule 673 T.C.P., then he shall be discharged. (Rule 666 T.C.P.) If the garnishee fails to file an answer to the writ of garnishment as required, the court may render judgment by default against the garnishee for the full amount of such judgment against the defendant. (Rule 667 T.C.P.) 

Should it appear from the answer of the garnishee that he is indebted to the defendant, the court will render judgment for the plaintiff against the garnishee. (Rule 668 T.C.P.) The garnishee, however, can set up whatever defenses he might have against the defendant (26 Tex. 283), and the rights of the garnishee in property held by him will not be prejudiced. (34 

Tex. 181.) If the garnishee fail or refuse to pay the judgment rendered against him, execution will issue thereon in the same manner and under the same conditions as is or may be provided for the issuance of execution in other cases. (Rule 668 T.C.P.) 

Rule 669 T.C.P. Judgment for Effects. 

Should it appear from the garnishee's answer, or otherwise, that the garnishee has in his possession, or had when the writ was served, any effects of the defendant liable to execution, including any certificates of stock in any corporation or joint stock company, the court shall render a decree ordering sale of such effects under execution in satisfaction of plaintiff's judgment and directing the garnishee to deliver them, or so much thereof as shall be necessary to satisfy plaintiff's judgment, to the proper officer for that purpose.

Rule 670 T.C.P. Refusal to Deliver Effects. 

Should the garnishee adjudged to have effects of the defendant in his possession, as provided in the preceding rule, fail or refuse to deliver them to the sheriff or constable on such demand, the officer shall immediately make return of such failure or refusal, whereupon on motion of the plaintiff, the garnishee shall be cited to show cause upon a date to be fixed by the court why he should not be attached for contempt of court for such failure or refusal. If the garnishee fails to show some good and sufficient excuse for such failure or refusal, he shall be fined for such contempt and imprisoned until he shall deliver such effects. 

Rule 672 T.C.P. Sale of Effects. 

The sale so ordered shall be conducted in all respects as other

sales of personal property under execution; and the officer making such sale shall execute a transfer of such effects or interest to the purchaser, with a brief recital of the judgment of the court under which the same was sold. 

SECTION 5-WRIT OF SEQUESTRATION DEFINITION 

The writ of sequestration is the means of seizing a specific piece of property in which the plaintiff asserts some right, title or interest, and which is in controversy, and placing it under the custody of the court pending the outcome of the lawsuit. 

ISSUANCE OF WRIT OF SEQUESTRATION Art. 6840 R.C.S. When to Be Issued. 

Judges and clerks of the district and county courts, and justices of the peace shall, at the commencement or during the progress of any civil suit, before final judgment, have power to issue writs of sequestration, returnable to their respective courts in the following cases: (a) When a married woman sues for divorce, and makes oath that she fears her husband will waste her separate property, or their common property, or the fruits or revenues produced by either, or that he will sell or otherwise dispose of the same so as to defraud her of her just rights, or remove the same out of the limits of the county during the pendency of the suit. (b) When a person sues for the title or possession of any personal property and makes oath that he fears the defendant or person in possession thereof will injure, ill-treat, waste or destroy such property, or remove the same out of the limits of the county during the pendency of the suit. (c) When a person sues for the foreclosure of a mortgage or the enforcement of a lien upon personal property of any description, and makes oath that he fears the defendant or person in possession thereof will injure, ill-treat, waste or destroy or remove the same out of the county during the pendency of the suit. (d) When any person sues for the title or possession of real property, and makes oath that he fears the defendant or person in possession thereof will make use of his possession to injure such party, or waste or convert to his own use the fruits or revenue produced by the same. (e) When any person sues for the title or possession of any property from which he has been ejected by force or violence, and makes oath of such fact.

ANCILLARY WRITS 

203 (f) When any person sues for the foreclosure of a mortgage or the enforcement of a lien on real estate, and makes oath that he fears the defendant or person in possession thereof will make use of such possession to injure such property, or waste or convert to his own use the timber, rents, fruits or revenue thereof. (g) When any person sues to try the title to any real property, or to remove cloud upon the title to such real property, or to foreclose a lien upon any such real property, or for a partition of real property and makes oath that the defendant or either of them in the event there be more than one defendant, is a nonresident of this state. 

Rule 696 T.C.P. Applicant's Affidavit.
